JOTTINGS BY AN OLD JOURNALIST.

Women on Farms.

The statement that farmers in the Lymm district are eagerly bidding for female labour, and the constant, appearance of similar items in newspapers, may easily give townspeople the impression—though nothing could be farther from the writer's intentions—that the employment of women in agriculture is some- thing new under the sun. Of course, the so- called weaker sex have always taken part in farm work, being particularly useful, indis- pensable indeed, during harvest operations. I have known women -walk three miles out to work, and back home 'after their day's toil in the fields. In last week's article headed "The Villages in War Time," Mr. Geo. A. Leach incidentally notes how, as the reaper ploughs its way, "the rabbits bolt from the dwindling cornfield square." In the cases of which I had experience such occasions furnished sport for two or three guns. It was not unusual to hand the rabbits over to the female workers, though I have seen a stout old Irishwoman disdain- fully refuse a couple as "too small entirely, and not worth the carrying away." \

No Pacifist Need Apply. As I read of the farewell of the Rev. H. T. Dixon, I thought of a young Congregationalist minister whom I met on a recent train journey. He confided to me that he was 29 years of age, and was only just entering upon his first pas- toral charge—an obscure little country church. He had had a successful college career, and had been commended to larger and more in- fluential churches having vacant pulpits, but when the officers learned that he belonged to what for convenience Lmay term the Dixonites they gave him plainly to understand that they had no use for him.

His Heart's Right There.. The reporter of the merq,orable scenes on the South Lancashires' departure from Bank Quay writes of "the emotion of Farewell, remember me," being "counterbalanced by ragtime." Just so. So far as I have ever been able to ascertain, our Tommies never sing "Home, sweet home," when on active service abroad. I questioned as to this a relative of my own who had been buried in his trench during a bombardment and so severely injured internally that he has been granted his dis- charge. Said he, "Now and again some joker would strike up the old familiar strains, but the fellows couldn't stand it, and the singer never got beyond the opening bar. Someone would cry out, 'Hello, there's another of 'em -wants his mammy,' and the warbler at once became, the target for whatever missiles were handy. None of us could stand 'Home, sweet home,' for a moment." •, , Aunty Guessed Right.

Something I read in a nephew's letter in last week's "Children's Corner" brought to my mind a rather amusing incident. I had called at a house where a little boy was visit- ing. He was playing in theirtteet, and when his aunt asked him to run round the corner on an errand, he did not immediately comply. "Run along," said she, "and perhaps the shopman will give you some biscuits." He complied with alacrity, and was served with the article for which he had asked. The grocer turned to the next customer, but the enter- prising youngster interposed with "Aunty said if I fetched her this you might give me some biscuits "—and he did. The confession was elicited by a surprised and somewhat scandal- ised aunty. • Quaint Epitaphs.

The capital example in last week's "Gossip" set me thinking of others. In his immortal "Elegy," Gray -favours the use of a Scriptural passage for an epitaph, but—well, tastes differ. I know of one in Waddington Churchyard, Yorkshire, in memory of an old boatswain who had had an adventurous career. The-was briefly indicated in rhyme, concluding • "And novr I-lie snug As a )3pg in a rug." This worthy died April 21st, 1789, but a few years after the said William (who ought to have been baptised George) Robey. Here is an instance of the use of poets' licence "Under the bouilif this ancient yew Lie the remains of Jonathan Blue. His name was Brown, but that wouldn't do!"

In Flitton Churchyard, Bedfordshire, is a still more amusing example of the kind. It runs— "Here lies the body of Mary ,Jpnes, Who died from eating cherry stones. Her name was really Smith; not Jones, But Smith wouldn't rhyme with cherry stones."

"The Present Moment." , This was the subject of the evening sermon at Wycliffe Congregational aurch on Sunday. The present moment—now—that ,which alone is ours, the past being gone, while the future may never _come. Methinks I could myself hold forth from this text. I 'should draw for illustration on Lord Avebury's "Peace and Happiness,' Z which has a chapter headed "Now." As Thomas b. Kempis hath it iu the incomparable "-Imitation," , "Now is the time to act, Now is the time to fight,

Now is the time to make myself a better man If to-day you are not ready, Will you be to-morrow?"

"Seize your opportunity," Pitta.cus—one of the seven wise men of Greece—was wont to say. Then there are the proverbs,- "Strike while the -iron's hot," "Make hay while the sun shines," "A stitch in time saves nine," and so forth and so on. And the Bible! "Teach me to number my days," said Moses. "Make me to know mine end, and the measure of my days," said David. "Sufficient unto the day," said Christ, "is the evil thereof." "The world's a bubble," says Bacon, "and the life of man less than a span"; as someone else puts it, "Genesis goes before, and Exodus is the next," therefore

"If it were done, when 'tis done, then 'twere well . It were done quickly." Archias, Governor of Thebes,, in the fourth century 8.C., received one day a letter of warn- ing, but gut it on one side, saying, "Business to-morrow," and lost his life in consequence. Lord Chesterfield said that the Duke of New- castle lost an hour in the morning and spent the rest of the day looking for it. Jeremy Taylor has his say upon this, of course, but let me hasten to a close with a quotation specially appropriate to these unprecedented times. "If a -man," said Bishop Fuller, "chance to die young, yet he lives long that lives well; a time misspent is not lived, but lost." However, I fear that few readers have accompanied me thus far, and that those who have struggled on are beginning to nod. That I be not fur- ther tedious I will say—Ta-ta, and promise not to offend in like manner again.

NERVE DRUGGING MUST STOP.

Government Takes Action.

Lack of sleep—weak nerves—worry—these are the three things which usually lead to the taking of cocaine, morphia, veronal and other terrible habit-forming drugs, the sale of which the Government has now prohibited. That this was a wise step is proved by the reports of eminent investigators, who claim that, except- ing in rare instances, the use of these drugs is entirely unnecessary. In fact, reports from all parts of the -United Kingdom prove conclu- sively that insomnia, nervousness, lack of energy, neurasthenia, &c., can all be relieved and cured by the use of a food phosphate known among chemists as bitro-phosphate, a 5-grain tablet of which should be taken with each meal; and accordingly chemists are being requested to advise clients suffering from weak nerves, insomnia, &c. to take bitro-phosphate instqad of drugs. It feeds the nerves and makes the& strong and steady. Vitality is raised to a high level, mental and physical lassitude are overcome; the brain acts quickly and clearly, and fatigue is less noticeable even after a most trying day. Many readers will appreciate the fact that handy pocket flasks, containing suffi- cient Bitro-phosphate tablets to last two weeks and costing hut 2/6, can now he obtained locally from 11. M. Ashton, 63, Sankey-street. Each flask contains a coupon guaranteeing satisfac- tiou or money back.

The liquor traffic has defenders but no de- fence.—Abraham Lincoln.

MEN, MUNITIONS & MONEY.

Heavy Penalty To Stop The Leakage Of Man Power.

EMPLOYING POTENTIAL

SOLDIERS.

A case of exceptional interest as showing the liability of firms who employ, either knowingly or otherwise, men available for military ser- vice, was heard at tile Borough Police Court on Thursday. The Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., were summoned by the Military Author- ities (Captain Arthur John MacPhail, recruit- ing officer, being named as complainant) for having, as alleged, knowingly employed men belOnging to the Army Reserve. Major Fox (St. Helens) appeared for the Military Authorities, and Mr. C W. Lee (Messrs. Henry Greenall and (Co.) defended. The summons set out the names of five men —Wilfrid Barrow., 36; •John Dolan, 29; Michael Hunt, 28; Arthur Marsh, 34; and Lauren,2e Chrism 21.. They were described as being absentees from tire Army without leave. Mr. Lee said the defendant company admitted a technical branch of the Act, and after he 'ease for the prosecution was stated he would address the Court in extenuation of *the offence.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0008.txt | included=true | included =====
"MAN POWER WE .MUST HAVE."

Major Fox said that in these times when there was a .plea of guilty it was thought it should be treated gently, but he was not so sure that it was his duty to leave it that way with the Bench. These cases were taken against the most iniportant.firm in Warrington so that by that means they (the military) would get an advertisement throughout the town, and in order that there would be no more offenses ct this description:, If their Worships inflicted the maximum penalty fine on the, firm then - would not be any unnecessary suffering and. loss, and if it was imposed it might result in their Worships not 'having any cases of that sort again. He was asked to leave it to their Worships to say what was the penalty to warn Warrington in future. The first man, Wilfrid Barrow, aged 36, was employed by the firm on of 'Ole 26th. He was brought before their Wor- ships and fined £2. John Dolan (29), wds also working for the firm for some considerable time, from 11th July. He had no exemption ticket, and was arrested and given over to the Army authorities. Michael Hunt, aged 28, employed by the firm, was also handed over^io the military authorities. The next two cases were slightly different. Arthur Marsh, aged 34, had not been brought before the Court because they had been slightly misled in the way he was taken on; and Laurence Chriscoli, 21, had. not been prosecuted. There the Court had young men fit for the Army and such a type of men that should not have been engaged by this firm. The country was in need of men of military age and fit. They must carry about with them their exemption certificate and their badge, and there was no difficulty in a firm asking, "If you are coming to work here, show me your exemption paper." The law is laid down on the point. They could not throw the whole responsibility from the firm's shoulders on the back of the men. It should be impressed upon military officers and military representatives that it was their duty to eet men for the Army, and that it was no part of their work to supply men for munitions, unless special arrangements were made by the authorities. It was their duty to get men for the Army and not to assist firms. The three factors by which this war was to be won were man power, munitions and money.' The man power comes first, and man power they should have as military representatives. Therefore they should use every effort to stop any leakage' of man power. , He asked the, Bench to inflict such a penalty as would stop this leakage in Warrington.

UNPARALLELED STRESS AND STRAIN. Mr. Lee, for the defendant company, said their Worships had personal knowledge of the fact that the firm employ' Over 2,500 persons, who had been working at very high pressure for many months past. In the stress and 'strain, unparalleled in the world's history, there were, he submitted, extenuating circumstances where so many thousands of hands were employed. The defendants might have erred, but they had erred in the public interests. The firm pleaded guilty and offered a sincere apology to their Worships. He might say the utmost vigilance was now being exercised by the defend- ant company. He added that there vas a combing out in May and June, and therefore, under all the circumstances, he appealed :to their Worships with, every confidence for leniency, and suggested that the justice of these five summoilses would be met by dismiss- ing them on pap /Sent of costs. The Chairman (Mr. W. Roberts) said the magistrates did not sew their way to dismiss the cases, because some of the men had been fined 40s. and they had been handed over. They thought that justice would be met by a fine of £5 in each case.

I NBADGED MEN.

'1 he Mayor On Firms Who Haw Been Keen On Badges.

When a comparatively young man named William John Martin, 297 Lovisly-lane, was charged at the Borough Police Court, on Mon- day, with failing to report himself for military service, Detective-Sergeant Reddington ex- plained that he was employed by. the British Aluminium Company, without any certificate of exemption. He had previously ben with Messrs. Hanson and Edwards, and on leaving them in April had to forego his badge. When spoken to about the matter, the manager of the British Aluminium Company said, he could not understand whY Martin had not applied fora badge.

The Alayor (Alderman- Peter Peacock) re marked that firms should regard it as an important point that on badged men leaving their employ they should notify the authori- ties. Employers, he added, were very keen on. getting their. men badged, yet when they left the employers would not take the trouble to tell the authorities.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0010.txt | included=true | included =====
Group Called Up, But Waiting.

Captain McPhail remarked that seeing there were so many cases of unexempted men being employed, he was instructed to ask for a heavier penalty than usual in, order to stop it. Martin said he had received no papers calling him up since he left Messrs. Hanson and Ed- wards. 14 had been waiting for them. , • The Mayor: You knew you were liable when you were not badged. Chief Constable Nicholls: His group has been called up months and months. Captain McPhail added that but for stopping the man in the street the military would have been out of touch with him owing to the firths not notifying them Fined AI and handed Over to the, military authorities.

LABOURERS FINED FOR: REFUSING TO OBEY ORDERS.

At the Munitions Court on Monday, two workmen in the employment of a firm at Sankey, were charged with having on several days in August refused to obey lawful orders. They were instructed to/ carry wire from one, part of the works to anot4r, but failed to clo so on two or three occasions. The representa- tive of the firm said that no reason was given for the refusal except on the part of one of them, who said he had "enough to do already." It was not extra work they were required to do as wire cleaners. If the company had not been shorthanded he might not have asked the defendants to do the work, but under the cir- cumstances he considered it their duty zo remove the wire. The duty of removing the wire had not been discharged for two months previous by another man, as suggested by de- fendants.

Defendant said they had always endeavoured to do their duty, and the wire had been taken to the place to which .they were told to ta'kp it. The President (Mr. T. J. Ridgway) said this was the first time this point had been raised. It was the duty of a labourer to obey orders, but the defendants would only have to pay 2s. 6d. each.

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 9, 1916.

WOUNDED IN THE ADVANeE.

LANCE-CORPORAL J. RICHARDSON.

Woundcd A Second Time And Loses A Limb.

On the 20th July, Lance-Corporal John Richardson, King's (Liverpool) Regiment, got hit on, the forehead and was treated for his wound in the stationary hospital at Rouen. The wound was, fortunately, a slight one, and in about a week's time he was sent out again to the trenches.

On August 29th, Mrs. Richardson, 44, Walton- road, Stockton Heath, received a letter from her husband stating that he eit hit in the leg at two o'clock on Sunday morning, 27th August, and that he had undergone an operation. He would 'be sent to a base hospital, and then prob- ably to England. The brave fellow did not mention the grave nature of his injuries, but on Sunday, 3rd September, Mrs. Richardson got a second letter from the General Hospital, Northampton, in which he told her that he left the base on Saturday morning and got to England the same /night. "I was completely done up when I got here," he went on, "but I have jiist had-my leg dressed and feel a bib better,' but, of course, far from well as yet." Then, having gently led up to the whole admission of his terrible ex- perience, he says. "I have had my left leg off and from what I can make out there is only a very little bit left. . . . . I am very lucky to get away with only the loss of my leg." He also stated that he might have to undergo a still further operation. .

Lance-Corporal Richardson, who is 37 years of age, enlisted in June, 1915, and was drafted out to France the-following Decein'aer. He was.

Vt'ounded. In The, Nose Close To Left Eye. • •

A War Office intimation which recently reached Mrs. Bentley, . 26, Wilson-street, in- formed her that her husband, Rifleman William Bentley, of the Kings Royal Rifles; has been admitted into No. 9' General Hospital,: Rourm, France, suffering from a-gun-shot wound. in •tie left hand. RIFLEMAN Wit. BENTLEY.

Wounded

however, under date August 24th,' to say he WaS wounded in the nose. The letter ran a!+ follows I am sorry to tell you that I am in hospital suffering from a shrapnel wound in the side of my nose, 'close 'to the left eye. It is not very much eonsidering, some people I have seen. I am glad it's rio worse, so don't worry yourself. ===== 0002601_19160909_art0014.txt | included=true | included =====
A JOCULAR DEFENDANT.

At the Munitions Court on Monday, before ~Mr. T. J. Ridgway and two assessors, a nuniiber of workmen were charged with,having absented

themselves from work on various dates, in July and August, without leave or giving any reason for so doing.

Fines were imposed ranging from 2s. 6d. to 21 Ws.

It transpired that the wages in some cases were £2 16s. per week, and even as high a .23 125., and some of the defaulters are single men.

In one case, the defendant, an elderly man, took _rather a jocular view of his position, and having pleaded guilty to losing time, was anxious to have the case decided quickly. Pro- ducing a very substantial linen bag, he created much amusement in Court by asking "What am I in your debt? "Tell me and I will pay. I want no humbug hie. Don't keep me wait- ing."

A representative of the firm said the man could ,earn £3 12s. a week. The Chairman told defendant he would be fined 10s.

Defendant: The expenses are heavy, aren't they? He then dived inta the bag and paid the amount in silver to the Clerk of the Court.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0015.txt | included=true | included =====
GIRLS' HIGH WAGES.

Another firm complained that an employe had stayed away from his work for several days in August. The man had been originally,paid 255. as a labourer, but he was now Able to earn from £2 to £2 10s. weekly. The firm's repre- sentative mentioned that girls employed at the same work were also earning these wages.—Thc Court considered this a very bad case and fined defendant £2.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0016.txt | included=true | included =====
BOWLING PlittASE.

Said He Would "Knock His Block

Off."

At the Munitions Court on Monday a boiler- maker who had been employed in a Warrington boiler works for seven years applied for an order granting him his certificate. The sub- stance of his complaint was that his 'foreman was always finding fault with him. He threat- ened to "knock his block off," and on another occasion caught him by the back of the neck and threatened he would smash him up. When applicant asked for his cards the foreman told him he would hake to appeal to the Tribunal. • The President (Mr. T. J. Ridgway): When he threatened to knock your block off, did he mean your work? Applicant: No, my head. (Laughter.) In answer to a member of the firm, whb sug- gested that applicant was wilfully causing this trouble in order to gO to another job for more nioney, the man, said it was not the money, it Was the comfort he wanted. He did not wish to work there again. The foreman admitted that he said to appli- cant," For two. pins I would knock your block, off," but he aggravated him' to say it. He made himself very disagreeable to him with the 'sole intention of getting him to say something. The certificate was granted.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0017.txt | included=true | included =====
COMPLAINT ABOUT WAGES.

At the Munitions Court on Monday a furnace hand in a local works asked for his certificate on the ground that his wages were not enough to keep his wife and family, and he wished to go elsewhere. He was paid 265. 6d. and 2s. bonus per week. He had not worked any overtime lately: A .representative •of the firm said that appli- cafit was paid a penny per hour more than the average rate paid-other abourers for the same duties. The work was entirely War Office work. If they could replace the man they would let him leave, but if he. left the. mill would close. The certificate was refused. -

===== 0002601_19160909_art0018.txt | included=true | included =====
A BOY'S APPLICATION TO THE MUNITIONS COURT. N

A boy, who had been in the employment of a local .coal firm, complained to the Munitions Court on. Monday that he had been dismissed without notice, and ,asked for compensation.

Mr. ' Harry Harrison', National- Union of General Labourers, presented the boy's case, and said the latter commenced work with the firm on the 27th July, his wages beirtg 75.. a week. On Saturday, August 19th, the pay clerk gave him his wages and his insurance card, and told him there was no more work for him to do, as the other boy was coming back. A member of the firm said the boy was not considered strong enough for the job. They would have given him a week's notice if he had asked for it. His was not a controlled firm,iand it was not usual to take or give notice in the case of casual labourers. Mr. Ridgway (president of the Courts held that the firm did not come within the Order made by the Ministry of Munitions of the 4th July, 1915. The application was, therefore, dis- missed.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0019.txt | included=true | included =====
" WIFE AWAY PLEASURING AT NEW BRIGHTON."

At Monday's Munitions Court an employe was summoned by a local firm for absenting himself from 'work at night and at'an important period of his duties. The mill was stopped for the time and they had to send out for another man. - Defendant said that his wife was away pleasur- ing at New Brighton, and he went away to get his supper. . The President said he would be fined 55., and added,'" Your wife ought to pay it for you."

CYCLING AND MOTORING.

HOW TO CLEAN A CAR.

It seems a far simpler operation to wash a ear than it is in reality. The washing is not so diffi- cult; it is in the drying and polishing where (Ale needs experience.. Those who are undertaking Una work for the first time this season—and there a" ma,ny who are, their chauffeurs being• engaged on more important work—will find the following hint! 'helpful:—Clean down the car after every ran a' soon as possible; on no account put it off till to; morrow, it is far more trouble then. Rubbing an dirt and dust is bad for the varnish and will ruin the best finish in no time. As the wheels, mud- guards and underside of the car are always dirtiest, wash these first, using all the pressure you can get on to the hose. When hosing has thoroughlY loosened the dirt, set to with a good bristly brush and finish off with another hosing. Use only gentle pressure of water to clean the body- panels and tops of the mudguards. When the water has so loosened the mud that it practically runs off, take the hose in your left hand and sponge in your right, and, working the two together, souse off the final stains. Never wash down the car in the sun; if you do it will dry patchy. When washing '5 finished, use a: clean leather to give the finishing touches. Treat the leather as if it were blotting Paper, using it to sop up surface moisture, rinsing it out in clean water and wringing it dry as soon as it becomes at all wet. Keep metal parts bright' If once they become really dull, it is ten times AS hard to brighten them again. THE TIMING OF THE MAGNETO.

Timing a magneto is 'a job at which many nict°r cyclists jib. True, it isn't perhaps one of the pleasantest tasks to perform, but it is certainlY job that every motor cyclist should be able to d° when necessary. First of all you want to have a clqpx knowledge as to when you want the spark t° our. There are three usual " positions " a sParifr ing, and these are: (1) When the piston is a frac- tion from the top position on its upward coraPr,es; sion stroke (when the exhaust valVe is closed, t°9'' is). This " position " is usually chosen for verY fast work. (2) When the piston is just at the tol? on the compression stroke; useful for all round work. (3) When the piston is a fraction of an inch from the top on the downward, or firing stroke; useful for heavy and sidecar work. Betwee!,. these three positions there is an infinite varietY suit the varying whims of various engines. then for the method. Remove the cover from tr driving gear and loose the chain wheel on 0° armature shaft shaft by unscrewing the lock-nut, ing a couple' of screw. drivers behind the cna'— wheel, and giving each a slight tap in turn. Leal° the chain in position. Open the 'engine conaPres- sion tap and with the aid of a bit of wire test the position of the piston. Move the engine Pn11(11 wheel until the piston is dead on top of. the ccAr; pression stroke '(when both valves are closed). 4e the spark control lever at two-thirds retarded, making sure the piston has not moved, turn tt: armature of the magneto until the points of n contact breaker are just about to break. Tne— tighten the lock-nut of ihe chain wheel and replace the gear case.

THE WAY TO CALCULATE HORSE-POWER!

The present methoa of stating the power 0 . motor car in terms of horse-power is far from sa r te factory. It is extremely misleading, and as a mat rø of fact is 'often quite erroneous. For instance, the may be two mdtor cars, one of which is rated a 7 h.p. and the other at, 12 h.p. Yet each may Pfut, sess the same cubic capacity and practicallY same power. The only difference' between the tw° is that one has a wide bore and a short stroke, whereas the other has a narrower bore and a long!! stroke. There have been suggestions made in 01! past for obviating this objection, but the followilu! appears to be the best solution Of the difficulty tlifo • to has yet been brought forward. The proposal Is the effect that the volume of gases swept out by tscei pistbns should be used as a basis. A good meth° would be to let 100 c.c. I epresent one unit of Power. On this basis, a 10 h.p, car, with a 1,500 c.c. eugine' would be rated at 15 h.p. This would in very Enfl cases be nearer the mark than the figure emPl°Y' at present.

MIXED BATHING.

Sir,--Now that mixed bathing is becoming so Popular one wonders why we in Warrington have not one or two days at the Baths set apart for the same purpose. I feel sue it would be the means of producing a healthier relationship'between the sexes, and the revenue Would be much greater. Of course it is too late this season, but next season the Baths Commit- tee ought to sanction its adoption the first Week. Men who know the art of swimming Would then be able to teach their sons and daughters how to swim. If this humble sug- gestion is acted upon I think the Corporation Would very soon see an increase in the receipts. Perhaps some of your correspondents who are interested in this matter will agitate until Something is done. .

Should Electricity Department

51,53 & 55, BRIDGE STREET,

WARRINGTON.

EXCLUDED AND ISOLATED.

Cairo Street Chapel Ignored by Roman, Anglican and Nonconformist.

MINISTER'S VINDICATION.

The Rev. Irvine Lister, speaking on "The Church Catholic," last Sunday morning, on entering upon the third year of his ministry at Cairo-street Chapel, said it -vas something for which he ought to be thankful that it had been possible to work out clearly for himself a complete and satisfactory conception of ' the Church, the need of its existence, its origin and power, its peculiar functions. and the primary object for which it existed. A Clear Conception Needed.

The .reverend gentleman having revieoed the claims of the Roman Catholic Church, and the various suggestions toward unity within the Anglican Church and the Nonconformist Churches, proceeded "If already there is so much movement and restatement within these great Churches, if they are all realising their weakness and shortcomings, as they undoubtedly are, surely it is incumbent on us also to re-examine our own position, and not to be satisfied until we have worked out a clear, simple and effective conception of our own Church. Why, after all do we exist? Why, in this town of so many Churches, do we hold an isolated position, and what is our real purpose in so continuing?

"Let me make clear to you the position we do hold, and, although I do not like to say this, I feel that it is necessary. We here, at Cairo- street Chapel, have no point of contact with the Roman Catholic Church, just as every other Church, Anglican or Free, has no point of con- tact. The Roman Catholic Church does not admit our right to teach Christian truth. It asserts that this which we call a Christian Church is not a Christian Church at all, and that we who are members of it are not members of the body of Christ. That attitude is clear and understandable, and it is uncompromising. "But 'so far as the Anglican Church is con- cerned, yiftk are in a similar position. There may not be the same abscilute denial of our right or power to teach Christian truth, there may be willingness to unite in philanthropic— which is Christian—work, but neither our ministry nor our membership is recognised. The Rev. R. J. Campbell, though ordained to the Congregational ministry, must retract his former teaching and beliefs and become reordained ; and members of Free Churches who would become communicants of the Church of England must be baptised. and confirmed before they can be received. So far as our own Church is concerned therefore, we are not con- sidered to be of the body of Christ. Oldest, Free Church Not A Free Church.

"But we have no point of "contact either with the so-called Free' Churches of this town. These Churches are called Nonconformist Churches, forming a body of their own, recog- nising one another's work and claims, and definitely united for various kinds of work in a Free Church Council. They have united especially for war work in various kinds of intercession and public services. It would seem,. therefore, as a Christian Church, hold- ing many of -the principles of government and church management which these other churches hold, that it would be possible for us to have fellowship with them. But we are excluded from their fellowship. The oldest Free Church in this town, with a long and honourable tradition, established when the 2,000 clergymen were ejected from the Church of England be- cause of the freedom they claimed, and served and upheld since then by some of the ablest ministers and scholars and the best citizens of Warrington, this Church, for a reason which, candidly, I cannot understand, is not invited to the fellowship of the Free Churches, and is not allowed representation on what is persistently called a Free Church Council. We are excluded from taking part in their- united efforts, and we are isolated. But, even more than that, although there exists a ,more or less informal gathering of Free Church ministers, who month by month meet for mutual help and fellowship, the minister of Cairo-street Chapel is not invited to attend, nor is his presence desired.; Here, again, in what is a body of no official standing, we have no point of contact with others; our work is not recog- nised by these Nonconformist ministers, and we have no fello*ship with them. Clearly, again, we are not recognised as belonging to the 'body of Christ.'

make clear that we occupy a position of isola-

"Now, I have mentioned. this in order to tion, and inasmuch as that is so, there is implied criticism and condemnation of our existence. The Romish Church contends quite frankly that we are heretics and have no justi- fication for our existence. The Anglican Church also holds that we are heretics, and have no right or power to teach Christian truth. And the so-called Free Churches, in- asmuch as they do not admit us to their fellowship, also 'plainly do not look upon us as a Christian Church, and they regard our claims as false. That is our position, a posi- tion we have held too long, and have too tamely acquiesced in. But, because we do hold this position, it is all the more incumbent on us to state clearly for ourselves and for others the reason of our existence, and our own con- ception of the true, Church of Christ. :We must justify. our -own position in order that we may individually stand against criticism, and in order that we convince others of the reasonableness of our claims.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0029.txt | included=true | included =====
The Supreme Revelation.

"This Church was founded after the great ejec- tion of 1662, anld those who founded it held that the only autholity- within the Church should be the Spirit of Jesus Christ, and that He, and not the State, was a sufficient guide. It is that belief which upholds our Church to-day: There has been no essential difference, and there has been no change in that belief or wavering from •it during the 259 years of its existence. This Church has stood for freedom of thought and belief, for the right to bring the intellect to bear upon the questions of faith, for the right to test creeds and dogmas by the light of truth, and for the right to allow the Spirit of Christ to make a progressive revelation, which, as we have had ability and power, we have accepted and fol- lowed. In the course of years, as progress has been madp in science and theology, we have been free from the fetters of tradition and dogma, and have been ready to receive the new

Milk and Typhoid Fever. Typhoid fever may be distributed throughout a district through the contamination of the milk supply. This is not because the cows are suffering from typhoid fever, for that has not yet been proved, but the water supply of the dairy may be infected with typhoid bacilli or one or more of the milkers or others who handle the milk may be the source of infection. It is dangerous to have on the premises of the dairy not only someone suffering from typhoid fever but also anyone who has had typhoid within two or three years, for it must be re- membered that even after what appears to be a full recovery from typhoid a person may still be carrying and distributing typhoid fever germs. Again the simplest remedy is boiling the milk or at least raising it to a temperature of 160 degrees Fahr. for some twenty minutes. —" Good Health." Corsets Cause Backache.

Writing in the September "Good Health," Dr. A. B. Olsen has the following to say about corsets and backaches: "In the case or women the wearing of stays , and corsets accounts for a very considerable number of distressing and persistent backaches. The pressure in the waist line has the effect of pushing the abdominar organs out of place and this causes a dragging and tugging of the 'supports, . which is certain to give rise to more of less ache and pain. Corset-wearing is a direct cause not only of pro- lapse oi the stomach, but also of the bowels. This meairs that there ais , a pulling down or dragging of the mesentery which supports these organs and an obstinate form of more or less distressing backache is the natural outcome. The con- striction of the waist, not infrequently affects the kidneys as well And causes one or even both of them to, be pressed out of place so that tnere is a movable or floating kidney. The stretch- ing and dragging of the nerves gives rise to much distress and backache that is sometimes well- nigh intolerable. It is much easier to get these organs out of place than it is to get them back to their normal position, so that prevention by absention from the use of constricting bands and corsets is the most satisfactory cure."

Vegetable Marrow and Cereal Salad. Fry a chopped onion in one tablespoonful oil, add one gill rice, heat well together and cover with twice the amount of vegetable stock or water, seascen with salt and cayenne or paprika. Cook with the lid tightly on for twenty minutes; drop loosely into a basin containing three tablespoonfuls of oil and one‘ef vinegar, salt, and pepper, and toss from time to time tilt cold. Cook the marrow till it is three-quarter done, and "cut into strips nioout ;In. thick. Season these well and dress in salad dressing highly seasoned. Mix with the rice, dress in a salad bowl, arrange tomato round it, and sprinkle with chopped herbs. Chicken Broth.

With every particle of fat or grease carefully taken off by blotting-paper when hot, may be drunk cold or iced, and will at times be re- tained when the stomach rejects everything else, especially in sea-sickness. It should be seasoned with a little salt, and taken from a glass feeder, when the patient is lying down. It is a refreshing and supporting drink, even in cases of fever, and should be given quite cold or iced, not luke-warm.

CHAT and GOSSIP.

By MARJORIE.

Queen Elizabsth as Nurse.

That the Queen of Roumania .and her daugh- ters will take an active part in the nursing of Roumanian wounded soldiers goes without say- ing, and in this they will be following closely in ,the footsteps of thd late Queen Elizabeth. In 1877 Roumania' joined Russia against Turkey, and in the war that followed the Princess Elizabeth, as she was then, and who had been remarkable for her intellectual attainlnents, dis- played great energy and resource in the organisa- tion of an ambulance corps of Roumanian ladies. She went through the task with a heroism that overcame all obstacles, and that greatly en- deared her to the nation. The Prince led his troops throughout the campaign, and his in- creased popularity after the war was due in great part to the untiring activity ,of his colt- sort. In 1881 the Prince was crowned King, and as he died leaving neither son nor daughter, Ms nephew Ferdinand succeeded him On the throne.

Feminine Insanity and the War. Since the latter part of 1914 there has been, according to an eminent medical authority, a sensible diminution in the number of women admitted to lunatic asylums. This is attributed to the change brought about by the war, in the daily lives of women of all' classes, but more especially of the middle classes. The sheltered. and unoccupied lives of the majority of the daughters of professional men and merchants, who still followed mid-Victorian ideas of gen- tility, stifled their psychic energies and induced neurasthenia and hypochondria. The war has set this class free from benumbing convention- alities, widened the outlook in life, and to a large extent banished the ennui of idleness, thus setting up an antidote to mental disorders. The diminished purchasing power of the sovereign, and the disappearance of domestic servants, have compelled the formerly comfortably-placed middle-clao housewife to set up a new stan- dard of living, to exercise an unwonted eccinomy, and to rely on her own initiative. Housekeeping' has become much more interest- ing in conseouence, and mental health has im- proved. Patchwork and Stitchery.

Many old faihions have been revived, and it is not surprising that many women are making patchwork of odds and ends of velvet and silk, and with the aid of end pieces of miffed silk, converting them into charming bolster cushions. The newest blouses are being made entirely by hand, and sewn together by means of pretty fancy stitches. Roumania, now an Ally of the Entente Powers, has given us a charming idea for openwork seams, used by the peasants in tbe making of their national costumes. Several other effective stitches coming from the same source may be used for securing hems. Knot- stitch, chainstitch, and faggoting are all worked in coarse thread. On the eddes of collars and cuffs beads and loop-stitches make a novel finish, and are a change fkom the. French knot that has been so long in use; loops of three or five beads being prettier than a large number. Blanket-stitch is also widely employed for the edging of collars and cuffs. In fact, during the holidays, handwork of a useful kind has been very much in evidence, and workbags have in- creased enormously in size ih order to accom- modate all the wools and implements required. Fashionable Footwear.

The fashion for the toilette of one colour. only, including millinery and footwear, has been recently revived, and has much to recommend it, as far as attractiveness is concerned. With regard to modish footwear, the cavalier shoe of patent leather with a :large-buckle, and worn with fine black silk stockings, is patronised by many well-dressed women at the moment. In fact, buckles have returned with a vengeance, and are worn in auaint old-world positions. Originally they were used as fastener& of the shoe strap, and some women now elect to use them thus; others place them on the instep, and

truth revealed to us. Thus, without any diffi- culties, the old false idea of the literal verbal inspiration of the Bible has 'given place to a more enlightened and reverent conception of the Scriptures, more in accordance with the truth. The ancient doctrine of the Atonement as a propitiation to an angry God has given place to the idea of a progressive redemption consistent with the love of God for rail Ms children. The conception of .a fallen and hope- less humanity has given place to a belief full of hope in the progressive development of mankind, and the crude idea of the Trinity, the Father uncreate, the Son uncreate, and the Holy Ghost uncreate,' has given" place to the reasonable and understandable conception of God—the Father of our Lord Jesus Christ and our Father. And in all these liberal and progressive changes we have not departed from the Spirit of Jesus Christ. His Spirit is authoritative in our Church. It breathes through our prayers, our hymns, and our music. It is proclaimed in all our ceremonies, and upon the -tablets on the wall, and He has this authority because He is to us The way, the truth and the life.' The only Church which can be Holy and Catholic is the Church Which is free to receive the truth as, in the wisdom of God, it is revealed to us. It is the Church which acknowledges the ever-pres- ent revelation of God in humanity as well as His 'supreme revelation in Jesus Christ,' and in which there is liberty of thought and con- Science."

Alas! your tears will not be stayed By, little words in rhyme; My imny thoughts are all afraid To trust themselves to Time; I think perhaps the Kaiser ,too Would scarcely heed my frown, And some might say my claims are few To weave the laurel crown: And so I take my knitting up and lay my pencil down.

I'll shape ,the toe and turn the heel, And vary ribs , and plains, And hope some soldier-man may feel The warmer for my pain; I'll fashion mitten, sock and glove In navy-blue and brown, And finish with a touch of love May call a blessing down: When I shall take my knitting up and lay my penril down, -

—" Punch."

£3OO LESS.

Guardians To Make Lower Call

On Ratepayers.

CROSS-TALK AT MEETING.

Although the estimates of expenditure for the Warrington Union for the half-year ending March next are £21,590 as against £20,962 for the corresponding period of last year, the ,Board of Guardians op Wednesday considered it was quite expedient to call upon the ratepayers for £17,590, a sum £3OO less than last year. At this time, said the Chairman (Mr. A. J. Jolley) it was extremely difficult to make really satisfactory. estimates owing to the fluctuation in the price of goods. They had decided to call for £3OO less, the balance they had in hand enabling them to do that.

The Rev. A. M. Mitchell said that the actual increased cost of the war budget was £628 more than last year, and that seemed a very large increase. He suggested that there might be a greater effort in the direction of economy. There were some small things which might be knocked out altogether or, at any rate, reduced. Take, for instance, deputation expenses, which amounted to £2O. He thought they ought to give up deputations during the war unless they paid their o*n bills. , Then as regards stationery there might be a reduction. The Clerk (Mr. A. Bottomley) pointed out that that had been reduced by a very large sum, much less than the price expended for 15 years past and, yet the price of material had gone up.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0035.txt | included=true | included =====

Mr. Mitchell,: I economise in every possible way by readdressing the same envelopes 1 receive.

Mr. George Mather: ,Do you use the same stamps? (Laughter.) Mr. Mitchell: Unfortunately, I cannot. Mr. Mather: But would you if you could?

Mr. Mitchell:, Certainly: and so would you. (Laughter, and hear, hear.) Continuing, he pointed out that "in-maintenance at the Work- house" last year cost .0,685, whereas now it was put at £5,000. The Chairman: Perhaps you will -exercise a little patience with Mr.-Mitchell, please. ,

Mr. Mitchell: We ought to have -a little more full information, and I appeal to the Clerk to satisfy ' Mr. Shaw: It's a farce.

THE EXAMINER,

SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 9, 1916.

LABOUR AND CAPITAL AFTER THE WAR.

At the Trades Union Congress this week at Birmingham two points full a satisfaction in respect Of the present and full of promise as to the future stood .out very prominently. The first is that the working men of this country have "cut " the. German Socialists— that is the working men of GermanST—till that class . has cut the KAISER and all his doings. The Parliamentary Committee of the Congress recommended the gathering to accept an invi- tation from the American Federation of Labqur to take part in an International Labour •Con—gress, simultaneously with the conference of belligerents which will settle the terms of peace, and they rejectO The pro- posal by a Majority of .two to one. This vote iof the delegates did not mean that they thought that Labour is not entitled to express its views on the terms of settlement. But they repudiated the suggestion of 'parleying, even at the latest stage of the war, with the Socialists of Germany and her Allies. Mr. G. H. ROBERTS, M.P., embodied the prevailing sentiment .in his declaration that " British workers would not sanction any negotiations with the German Social Democrats or their Government until the German democracy had purged itself of Kaiserdom and all that it stood for." The Congress did not commit themselves for the future one way or another; they simply stated briefly, but emphatically, that the workers of this country will have nothing to do with the workers of Germany until Belgium and France have been cleared

of their other and 'guilt-stained "comrades."

The other subject which is matter for con- gratulation is that the workers see that Labour conditions will alter after the war, and that arrangements will have to be niade in keeping with the new conditions if we are to triumph in that industrial struggle which is to fodow the struggle in arms. The em- ployers also see this, and the LORD MAYOR of BIRMINGHAM (Mr. NEVILLE CHAMBERLAIN), who is a good representative of the class and welcomed the Congress to the great Midlind city, indicated most clearly that Masters and men may, if they like, be brought into closer touch and harmony, to their inutual benefit and the lasting good of tho ,country. After the frightful waste of the war we shall have to produce more, and the way in which we have produced more, while we have spared five or six millions of men for fighting pur- poses, Droves that we have great reserves of strength that have previously been running to waste. Certainly we do not desire to be kept at the present straining point perpetually; but with improved methods of organisation and the adoption of iiiodern machinery there is no reason why this 'country, should not in- crease its output, and therefore its wealth, enormously. There should be no limitation of output; and, of course, on the -Othest. hand, there should eno cutting down of wages or privileges. The• Conference leaders and Mr. CHAMBERLAIN were at one on these points. Both agreed that tile men and masters should work more in co-oPeration Mr. CHAMBERLAIN going so far as to say that the men's leaders should be allowed "to see a little more of the gam v " of trade " from the inside." He foreshadowed a number of.demands which the workers, " who now foimed the bulk of the Army,' would reasonably make after the war. They were : (1) A wage which would give them a ' reasonable margin over the cost ot living;

(2) Regularity of employment; (3) Improved conditions in factories and homes, so that the workers could preserve their health and spirits and. bring up their chirdren in cheerful and healthy surroundings. On the other hand, Mr. CHAMBERLAIN, said, employers would 'want : (1) Production kept up to the highest • point of efficiency in return for shorter hours and better wages; .(2) Reasonable notice of demands for changes of wages; (3) Security that labour would not be unreasonably withdrawn.

There will be plenty to do in the days that are coming, and we shall have to do it to meet the wastage that has been occasioned, and to meet the burden of taxation that is being forced upon us. Conunon-sense dictates that we should bend our efforts to the task of dis- covering the most profitable way in which we can get the work done; not in adding to the waste by either side, or both, disputing about privileges or rights that, properly approached, could be readily adjusted. The spirit of reasonableness whial pervaded. the Congress is certainly a good omen.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0046.txt | included=true | included =====
GOSSIP.

Some Wounded German prisoners were con- veyed through Warrington in motor cars on Sunday morning.

On Liverpool-road a• woman threw two packets of cigarettes-into the car as it passed by. A man who saw her said, "Don't you know they are Germans?" "Well, thy are some mothers' lads for all that," she replied.

After visiting Raddon Court Hospital on Wed- nesday afternoon, her Grace Katharine Duchess of Westminster crossed the road to The HoHies and took tea with Mrs. Broa,dbent and family, who are devoted to Red Cross work.

&The Duchess then left by motor car for Bank 7uay Station, returning by rail to her home, Cambermere Abbey, near Nantwich.

The company producing "Knick-Knacks" at the Royal Court Theatre had an exhauStinf; time at the beginning of this week.,

Due to arrive in Warrington at 3 o'clock on Monday morning from a North-Eastern town, they were held up on the way by the Zeppelin raid, and their train .steamed into, our station at 7.30. •

Most 6f them were unable to get any sleep before the matinee began and then had to give two shows later in the day under the same conditions.

, As they performed they displayed the normal vigour- and vivacity, but they were thankful when their task was o'er and could take to their beds for .sorrie more beauty sleep.

Although the Rev. H. T. Dixon left Perketh only on the 31st August, the " W(,sleyan Methodist Magazine" fo Septemir.i has twc lines struck across his name on the cover and his successor's name, the Rev. F.; Hudson, printed underneath. The name of the Rev. Joseph' Howard, who died: several months ago, however, still stands Apmolested.

Why was no reference made at the Town Council meeting to the late Mr. Henry Isherwood, the well-known journalist, Who, on Tuesday, through death, missed his first meet- ing for probably thirty years?

Those, at any rate, who were helped to their seats by his journalistic advocacy /night have ventured for him an epitaph when he had in- scribed for them a prologue.

Councillor Wilson says those people who bump into unlighted lamps at night have grounds for action against the Corporation,:

I think the Councillor is wrong. The lamps go unlighted by order of the Defence of the Realm Act, and the people know the lamp- posts are the-re. ,

We have to risk one or two noses that the town may escape. Still, the Corp Oration might make our way home in the (lark ,as little dan- gerous as possible.

Few people will disagree with Councillor WU- son's suggestion that lamp-posts should be painted white so that we shall be able-to detect their gaunt .forms in our darkeneci streets. Bumping into an iron pillar is no joke.

And while the Gas Committee are engaged in painting the lamp-posts, :the Paving Committee might whiten the edges of our. footpaths: Those who get shaken by an unexpected descent into the gutter will mit be inclined-to whitewash the Committee when in need.

If I had my choice I would prefer knocking my head against a• lamp-post I did:not know was there, than "ricking" my back by stepping on to a footpath which is not there, while I thought it was. Perhaps I am stronger-headed "than backed.

VIGILANT.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0047.txt | included=true | included =====
THE HEROIC STRAIN.

Sir Arthur Crosfield's Notable Poetic Achievement.

A Pilgrimage of the Empire. By Sir Arthur Crosfield, Bart. London: Eyre and Spottis-

woode. is. and 2s. 6d. net

The author, says the "Liverpool Daily Post," having made a tour of the British Empire(has given us his impressions in poetic form, and one cannot but express surprise at the success which he achieves. The Spenserian stanza is a form of gua difficulty which even experienf.:ed poets hesitate to use, for, as Shelley remaiked, one either magnificently succeeds or dismally fails; there is no• third course. The Spenserian form demands a large-- vocabulary and a great reserve of emotional force, and Sir Arthur Crosfield, although lie falls heavily now and again, has achieved a flight with such ease and .success that we doubt whether there is any, con- temporary who could sifrpass him within the limitations of the stanzaic form. It is a poem full of national fervour, and at times soars on the wings of the Muse;" the poet's review of history has breadth as well as spirit.

Grim seer of ancient Egypt! Thou best seen These mighty empires which have passed away. Wilt not thou here bear witness all have been Controlled by force, beneath despotic sway Subdued? Yes, in this long and sad array Of empires all subverted—effort spent And wasted—there is still but one that may Claim that in polity and government Slavery hath been abjured and help to freedom lent. ,

Must this too pass—may we not hand it down, Each generation cherishing 'the pan, And striving for the future and renown And honour through its territories vast? Great principles of liberty held fast, And qualified by' prudence and the sense Of wide responsibility, shall las. ThroUghout the ages while deep referen,- For justice fills our hearts with loyalty intenie.

There is nothing lame in those lines, and they are, often equalled, and the poem marches on to a stately ending.

Shoulder to shoulder through the shades of night, Together they ,have fought, but earliest dawn Now glimmers in the East with fairy light And heralds golden glory of the morn When Peace on Earth shall yet again De born. Then shall our warriors in their xosts return, With glory of achievement to adorn Their triumph, from all kith and kin to earn The gratitude, and pride with which all hearts shall burn.

The entire profits from the sale of the book will l given to Sir Arthur Pearson's fund for tkiose blinded in the war. Sir Arthur Crosfield has accomplished his task successfully, and the fruits of it could not be devoted to a better cause.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0048.txt | included=true | included =====
A Short Rule For Thrift.

A Yorkshire maxim mentioned to me by the late Lord Crimthorpe may be a more practical guide to thrift than much of the curl-ent exhortation. He said: "You don't understand thrift in the south. I believe you .have maxim which may be good enough for you: 'Don't buy things when you don't want them, but wait till you do.' But we have a much better one in the north: 'Don't buy things when you do want titem, but wait till you don't.' "—Dean Wace.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0049.txt | included=true | included =====
When Her Heart Failed Her.

As a' hospital nurse sat on a railway platform waiting Thr her train, a portly country dame waddled towards her. With a heavy sigh of relief she sank down on the seat and disposed of her parcels' and umbrella. Then she started chatting. " Ah," she said, eyeing the nurse's uniform admiringly, "I don't know what we'd do wi'out the likes o' you." ' "Oh, now, you're too kind," protested the nurse, with a smile. "I'm quite sure you' do things its worthy every day." "Not me, miss," replied the old lady mourn- fully. "I can kill a duck or fowl wi'-the best, that I'll admit, but when it comes•to 'uman beings my 'eart fails Me."

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 9, 1916.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0051.txt | included=true | included =====
ALLEGED ENEMY GOODS, I PERMANENTLY CRIPPLED! GIFT OF AMBULANCE.

A Warrington Dealer's Heavy Loss Through Confiscation.

THE ONUS OF PROOF.

A case of far-reaching importance to those who deal in goods of foreign origin was heard before the Stipendiary magistrate at the Grimsby Police Court, 'Friday last week, when William Furnival, general dealer, 53, Bewsey- street, Warrington, was summoned to show cause why five cases containing incandescent gas mantles, which were seized at Grimsby, should not be condemned as being of enemy origin. Mr. F. Dart appeared on behalf of the Customs and Excise Department, and. Mr. H. J. Westbrook (Messrs. Robert Davies and Co., Warrington) represented Mr. Furnival.

ASSUMED TO BE OF ENEMY ORIGIN. Mr. Dart at the outset explained that the case was not a criminal one, but the proceedings were taken under the, Customs (War Powers) Act; 1915, which gave the Customs Commissioners power to seize any goods imported into the United Kingdom which they Shad reason,to sus- pect were of enemy origin. All he (Mr. Dart) had to do was to prove the seizure, and it was for his friend (Mr. Westbrook) to prove, as re- quired by the Act, that the goods were not of enemy origin. These mantles were landed at 'Grimsby last December from a Rotterdam steamer. The consighors were the South Hol- land Mantle Co., and the consignee Mi. Furni- val. The mantles were seen by Mr. Stoddart, an'officer of Ctstoms, and samples were vnt to Londoil. The Foreign Trade Department in London found that the mantles were sewn with a machine which the South Holland Company did not possess, and accordingly Maintained that the goods were of enemy origin. He did not at all question- the bona-fides of Mr. Furnival, but if it were merely suspected that the goods were of enemy origin they must be forfeited, unless neutral origin were Proved. He recognised that his friend had a difficult task to perform. '

===== 0002601_19160909_art0052.txt | included=true | included =====
MR. WESTBROOK CANNOT PROVE A NEGATIVE.,

Mr. Westbrook said it would be seen that he Was required by the Statute practically to prove a negative, a task which even-the great Euclid. did not attempt, though he showed that several conclusions were absurd. He (Mr. Westbrook) might be able to show an absurdity in this case. The • authorities in one of .their letters stated that the mantles had been examined and were found to be. sewn by a machine which they alleged these mantle makers iii Rotterdam did riot possess..., On this the authorities concluded that nd such machine was possessed by anyone in Holland, and therefore the sewing must have been done in an enemy country. He submitted this was rather absurd. As a Matter of fact, the consignors stated that they did possess such a machine, but, of course, their evidence would not be accepted. Although the amount which Furnival had paid was a considerable one, it was not sufficiently large to justify the en- gagement of expert witnesses or the bringing over of witnesses from Holland. He, therefore, relied, upon the certificate of origin which the British Consul at Rotterdam had given on the bill of lading, ,and on the faith of which Mr. Furnival had parted with his money. That cer- tificate clearly stated that the Consul had been satisfied by evidence, documentaity and. other- wise, that these goods were fit for importation into England—in Other words, that they were not of enemy origin. .If the certificate were of any value at all it ought to be sufficient to pro- tect'an English trader who, as Mr. Furnival'had done, had bona fide' dealt in goods of this sort. Mr. Furnival had taken all reasonable steps and precautions to satisfy the requirements of the law. If these mantles Were forfeited the disease which _the Act sought to remedy would not be remedied because the money had already reached its destination and could not be re- covered.. Consequently the British subject would be punished and the enemy 'trader (if there were one in this case): would go scot free. He (Mr. Westbrook) relied upon the Consul's certificate as sufficient evidence in Mr. Furni- val's favour. The Dutch firm who sad to Mr. Furnival were not put on the "black list" until February; ).916; so that Mr. Furnival, who bought the goods in November last, could not be aware that he was dealing with a suspected firm of traders. , - -

Mr. Furnival deposed that he ordered the mantles from a Mr. Stein, of Manchester, with whom he had had dealings for twenty years and whom he believed to be a straightforward man.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0053.txt | included=true | included =====
NOT PROVED THAT GOODS WERE NOT OF ENEMY ORIGIN.

The Stipendiary said the goods must be for- feited, as Mr. Furnival had not proved they were not of enemy origin. It had been within Mr. Furnival's power to produce witnesses from Holland to prove that the goods were made in Holland, but he had not done so. He re- gretted he had paid for the goods, but that had nothing to do with the point at issue. He sympathised with Mr. Furnival and hoped that in some way or another he might recover his money. Mr. Dart then appried for costs, especially of the expert witnesses he had brought down. Mr. Westbrook strongly opposed this, , saying the authorities had sufficiently punished his client by seizing his goods. Moreover, the ex- pert witnesses had not been called.' The Stipendiary said he would not grant costs against Mr. Furnival.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0054.txt | included=true | included =====
PEARSON & KNOWLES.

The Dividend And Bonus To Be

Paid This Year.

. The directors of the Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Co., Ltd., have decided to re- coipmend the payment of the usual half-year's dividend at the rate of six per cent. per annum on both the preference and -second preference shares, and a dividend of 6s. 6d. per share on the ordinary shares, making 10 per cent. for the year, all less tax. They also recommend a bonus on the ordin- ary shares of ss. per share, free of Income Tax. In consequence of the amount of excess pro- fits and munitions duty not having been settled, the directors are deferring recommen- dations to reserve fund, and the meeting at the end of the month will be held proforma only, and adjourned until the accounts are finally settled and submitted to the share- holders.

Pearson and Knowles' Good Year.

The Pearson and Knowles Coal and Iron Company,' says the " Mauchester Guardian," has apparently had one of the best years in its history, for the ordinary dividend recom- mended, including the 5 per cent. bonus, will make 15 per cent. for the year, as against 71 per cent. for 1914-15, 5 per cent. for 1913-14, 8 per cent. for 1912-13, and rates ranging from 31 to 111 per cent. for eight preceding years. The company ' did well in -1914-15, the profit shown being .2122,359, which compared, with £98,812 in 1913-1, .1118,870 in 1912-13, sums ranging from .£61,890 to £70,978 in three pre- ceding years, and £116,010 in 1907-8, when the 11i per cent. distribution was made. Last year, it may be remembered, the reserve was increased by £47,500, making) it £450,000, as against an ordinary share capital of £610,000. The sum to be put,to the fund this year cannot he fixed at Present, as the amount payable •in l taxes has not yet been settled, and the com- plete accounts cannot be Submitted to the share- holder s until later. It is. evidently contem- plated, however, that there will be something for the reserve after the Government's claims have been met. The directors are thus con- tinuing the policy of strengthening their finances, which must haye been a great help to them in increasing the profits. In the last three years alone they .have put 117,500 to -the' reserve, and they have been fortunate in their holding of the 250,000 ordinary shares of the Partington Steel and Iron Company, which came into the field at an opportune moment and was able to pay 10 per cent. for 1914-15.

Duchess Assists At Effort To

Lighten Their Burden.

APPLETON HALL FETE.

Her Grace, Katharine, Duchess of West- minster, declared a garden fete open in the beautiful grounds of Appleton Hall on Thurs- day afternoon, the object being to aid Lord Roberts's Memorial Fund fc,r Disabled Soldio.rs' Workshops. Heaven has smiled literally upon every local effort recently made on behalf of our wounded soldiers, and Thursday afternoon was no exception. Mrs. Lyon, who organised the fete with the untiring help of Mr. H. Beresford Melville, hon. secretary and treasurer, must have been delighted with the appearance of her chanting lawn, spread with tents in which eager helpers were busy all the afternoon. The at- tendance was most encouraging, and amongst the company we noticed t n addition to many 'others actively engaged in the fete: .Colonel Lyon, Lieutenant Francis C. Lyon (Grenadier Guards), Lady Harmood Banner, Mrs. J. Charl- ton Parr, Mrs. Rylands (Thelwall), Mrs. Broad- bent and the Misses Broadbent, Mrs. Brereton Fairclough, the Rev. Canon and Mrs. Willis, the Rev. the Hon. C. F. Cross, the Rev. H. V. Pigot, the Rev. J. Collins, the Rev. 11. G. Thompson, the Mayor of Warrington (Alderman Peter Peacock), Messrs. Hy. Greenall, L. G. Davies, Jos. Harrison.

Tea tables were laid in, the open, and tea under such circumstances was very enjoyable. It was difficult to find a vacant chair. Concerts were given in a large marquee by willin3 helpers—Mrs. W. B. Robeits's- orchestral band played selections under her direction—and dancing was permitted on the velvet croquet ground. The sylvan surroundings added new attraction to the gipsy fortune tellers, and various outdoor amusements were . well patronised. Potato and egg races for ladies occasioned a great deal of merriment. The various tents were in charge of the following ladies :--

Produce Tent.—Mrs. R. Tickle, Mrs. T. Ditch- field, Mrs. J. Whitlow, and other ladies. Tea Tent.—Mrs. Armitage (Walton Lea), Mrs. H. Beresford Melville, Mrs. Segar Owen, Mrs. J. J. Whitley, and others. ' Flower Tent. —Lady Harmood• Banner, Mrs. Henry Greenall, Mrs.. Frank Cooper, Mrs. Percy Mortimer. and others. Cake Pent.—Mrs. Bower, and others.,

Basket Stall.—Mr. Leslie Melville. The amusements were conducted under the superintendence of Lieutenant Frank Lyon. • Mr. McConnell, tent-roz‘ker, of Warrington, lent the tents at a special price ,quoted on account of the war. ,

===== 0002601_19160909_art0057.txt | included=true | included =====
MAN'S DESIRE TO WORK.

Mrs. Lyon, in explaining the object of the fete, said that some few weeks ago an earnest appeal reached her from the headquarters of the Lord Roberts's Memorial Fund for Disabled Soldiers' and Sailors' Workshops-100,000 was needed at once to extend workshops for dis- abled soldiers and sailors. Disabled men ,dis- charged from the Army up to .Deceinber, 1915, numbered 32,000. This number was daily on the increase, and, of course, must be much greater now. These men received a Government pen- sion, it Was true, but to those who had given U p well-paid posts at home to fight for their country this pension was quite inadequate. Moreover, no pension could compensate any man for the loss. of his activity. It was difficult for those who were able-bodied people to realise the tragedy .of being permanently crippled. From the beginning men were born to work, and though in these days' women were compelled to take their places, and certainly had done their part, the natural order of things could never entirely be reversed. A desire to work must and should always be a normal and 'healthy attribute of manhood. Consider, then, from a man's point of view, how terrible must be the prospect of a long life of uselessness. Thousands' of men were facing this to-day who had come forward to their country's need. To help to fipa them tseful 'work and occupation which would give them a sense of independence and self-respect was surely the least they could do. The scheme to provide these workshops was started., on the close of the South African War. They were described as light, airy factories, specially equipped with machinesjidapted to the use of the maimed. Some cOuld be work' x 1 by one- armed men, others were for legless men, and others for less partially paralysed. These workshops, , when once started, pay their own way and ever ensure permanent employment. Needless to say, in these days- of shortage of men, tha value of this work when well organ- ised cannot be over-estimated, particularly when we take into consideration that many cheap articles made in Germany in former days can now be thus supplied. She sincerely hoped that they would make a sufficient sum to con- tribute well towards these many needs. The Lord Roberts's Memorial Fund for Disabled Soldiers' and Sailors' Workshops was incorpor- ated with the Soldiers' and Sailors' Aid Society, of which Katharine Duchess of Westminster was president for Cheshire. She felt sure they would join is expressing to her Grace the great please it was 'to see .her amongst them that day. They fully appreciated her kindness in coming to open the fete, and were also very grateful for the interest she took in their branch of the Needlework Guild, of which her Grace was Lady President. She then asked the Duchess to open the fete.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0058.txt | included=true | included =====
DUCHESS 'SAYS "NO CHARITY."

The Duehess of Westminster , said this great undertaking must appeal closely to 'everyone. Their object was' to.help to provide their gallant soldiers with emplbyment so that they should not be an object for charity, and that they would be able to hold up their heads as high as they ever did. That was what the Lord Roberts's homes were built for. Her Grace •then referred to the Needlework Guild which was started man) ~,,ears ago, and the Daresbury Division had provided useful garments for the poor in many parts of Cheshire. It was hard to keep up the appeal perhaps as their sympathies were more inclined to help those who are fighting for us, and the hospitals needed subscriptions, and they could hardly hope to have the number of woikers for the Guild as great as formerly. But if it were abolished now it might be difficult to make people\ feel the same interest a ney had done in time past. Mrs. Lyon had also mentioned the prisoners of war. She did not think there was an appeal ever made that received so nateAL heartfelt help as that fund had done, and they probably -knew people in that division who had been kind enough to adopt prisoners of war. They heard how grateful the men were for these parcels which' were sent them. It was yery doubtful if only for these parcels many of them would not be able to 'return to this country. She had had very grateful letters from those to whom she had the privilege Gf sending parcels. ,It was'encouraging to know that the effort made to help our prisoners_ of war had not been lost, because she thought that in every• case the parcels had been receivEd, and one could only know by their messages how pinch they are appreciated.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0059.txt | included=true | included =====
HER GRACE'S GOOD DEEDS.

Her Grace then declared the fete open, and hoped it would be, in every way successful. Rev. the Hon. C. F. Cross, vicar of Stretton, who proposed a vote of thanks to the Duchess of Westminster, did so in very graceful terms, -likening her many good deeds to the guided sheaves of corn. He mentioned the fact that on the previous day the, Duchess had been at Raddon Court, and on the morrow he would be at Chester, again doing good. (Applause.) Lady Harmood Banner seconded the vote, which was passed very heartily. Rev. H. G. Thompson, vicar of Appleton Thorn, proposed that thanks be given to Mrs. Lyon for throwing her grounds open for the pur- pose' of the fete and for her interest in work it was organised to support.

Miss Broadbent seconded. Mrs. Lyon -having acknowledged the corapli- ment, Colonel Lyon conveyed the appreciation of Mrs. Lyon to all the workers for the great assistance they had given, and this was accentuated by all present. This concluded the formal part of the open- ing ceremony, and the Duchess of Westminster was presented with a choice-bouquet of orchids by Miss Melville. "What beautiful firers!" said her Grace.
