Duchess On Warrington's

Kindness.

BUT MORE NEEDED.

Standing within a few yards Of a £4OO motor ambulance drawn up in the grounds of Raddon Court Hospital on Wednesday afternoon, her Grace Katharine Duchess of Westminster, on behalf of the Cheshire Branch of the British Red Cross Society, of which she is president, accepted it gratefully and graciously as a gift from the inhabitants of Warrington and dis- trict.

Interesting as the ceremony was in itself, by reason of the presence of several distinguished people, what it symbolised deepened its grandeur: the ambulance was a token of and memorial to our soldiers caught by the enemy's steel during acts of gallantry.

• Before her Grace- arrived with CAonel Sir Edward Cotton-Jodrell, county director of the 8.R.C.5., about sixty nurses were formed three or four deep on the lawn ready for inspection, about as many wounged soldiers sat on the grass, and twice as many of the general public found accommodation on forms. As her Grace drove up in Mr. W. Peter Rylands's motor car labelled "8.R.C.5.," the nurses and soldiers stood at attention, and civilian ladies closed their sunshades one after the other, so that those,behind &mild obtain a clearer view. The Duchess looked charming. Her erect and courtly figure was dressed in black, the V of th.a bodice being filled in with cream netting and ornamented with a diamond pendant. Through her black lace mantle, as tbe sun behind feint clouds, shimmered her white satin sash worked with a red cross and indicating her office of president. Miss Sylvia Broadbent, president of the Daresbury Division of the Societe, took the chair, and among those present were Mr. Bry.don, county secretary; Miss Margaret Broadbent (commandant at Raddon Court), Cplonel and Mrs. Lyon, Mrs. J. C. Parr, Mrs. Beresford Melville, Mrs. and Miss 'Constance Broadbent, Alderman Bennett and Mr. J. E. Birtles, in addition io the following township leaders: Mesdames Fox (Warrington), Segar Owen (Stockton Heath), IDakin (Latchford). Pigot (Grappenhol), Dar- wall (Walton) and Bower (Stretton). Miss Sylvia Broadbent made the presenta- tion, asking her Grace to be so kind as T,) accept the ambulance as a gift from the Dares- bury Division, the only request, approved by the County Director, being that after the war it should be retained in Warrington for civilian purposes, there being many large worl:s in the neighbourhood and accidents frequently occur- ring: The money for the ambnlarce had been collected by Mrs. Fox and herself, and they had ,the greatest pleasure in offering• the result et their labours to the B.R.C.S. in the hope that it would be of service to our brave soldiers. (Applause.)

===== 0002601_19160909_art0062.txt | included=true | included =====
THE GIFT ACCEPTED.

Rising from a chair .and 'still holding' her black sunshade, to. protect her from the heat, the Duchess uttered very suitable words for„the occasion. She expressed her very great'pleasure in coming to Warrington to accept the new ambulance, which she heard. had already been put to the use far which it was intended. Ambulances were badly needed at this moinent, and she was very glad that Cheshire and that part of . Lancashire were not behind in their generosity. AmbulanCes were of great- assist- ance to the Red Cross tHospitals, which were doing such wonderful Iwork ,throUghout the country. She was glad to hear that in times o!! peace the ambulance would also be used for a good purpose: that -of taking those who were injured to the hospitals. She wished for the ambulance a successful career, and again ex- pressed her gratitude to the donors for ,having now given it to the Cheshire branch. (Ap- plause.)

===== 0002601_19160909_art0063.txt | included=true | included =====
AN EXAMPLE TO WARRINGTON.

Colonel Sir Edward Cotton-Jodrell also ex- pressed his gratitude, as County Director, for the ambulance. One or two facts which he would present would show the nec(d they had for vehicles of that description. 'When the war started two years ago they had to provide a transport system all of a heap, so to speak. Directly the retreat from Mons began the wounded began to flow in, and it was a question of geting together transport to receive them in less than forty-eight hours. In Manchester, by dint of very hard work on the part of volun- tary helpers and also by the patriotic way private owners came forward, they were able in a very short time to get together an amount of transport which was just equal to demands. Since then things had moved very quickly and much development had taken place, with the result that in Manchester they had now about 229 ambulances, and between one and two hundred cars constantly at their disposal. In the last few weeks these had been practically alwiiys at work. In -other parts of the county they had now affiliated to the B.R.C.S. a trans- port section at Birkenhead which possessed about twenty ambulances and a large number of cars as well. In Chester, at one time, they were in very narrow straits indeed, but he was happy to say there had been a great improve- ment, and they had now six ambulances actually on the road in that district. The reason he was telling 'them these facts was that, while he was extremely grateful to those who had provided that one ambulance from.. this dis- trict, it was no use mincing matters, for, like Oliyer Twist, he wanted more—he wanted more froiq Warrington.,

The B.R.C.S. had not yet, got the full number of ambulances they would like. Some cars badly needed rest and repair, but could not be attended to because they were always needed for service. If Chester and Birkenhead could do so well, surely Warrington could also do better. He hoped that those who had not contributed to the provision of that motor ambulanee—and there were Many—would come forward and raise up some more ambulances, because they were badly needed. He was not asking them to do that in order to make a big record for the county—he did not want to be better than his neighbours—but the necessity was there and must be attended to, as they must never be at a loss for ouick and adeauate means of transfer- ring men from one place to another as long as the war lasted. (Applause.) In thanking the Daresbury Division he would like to couple with that the name /of Mr. J. E. Birtles, who had done so Much in the matter of motor transport of the wounded in this district, he being the local commandant. (Applause.) Colonel Lyon, who is over on 'leave, from France for a few days, hoped that that gather- ing would join in expressing their heartiest thanks to her Grace for being so good as to come and accept the ambulance. A larger view might be taken of her Grace's activity, for by her work she personified all- the work of the women of England, and, speaking in the name of the Army, he said they thoroughly appreciated it. They were particularly grateful for the labour of women on munitions and in the hospitals. Without them the Great Push would have been impossible. "(A pplause.) • WHAT SOLDIERS THINK OF US.

In seconding, Alderman Bennett expressed his regret for the absence of the Mayor, who was engaged on important business elsewhere. Since the war began there had been fifty-two convoys received in Warrington, and the treatment the men received in the hospitals in the district was fully appreciated. A few days ago in the shades of Carnarvon Castle he came across two soldiers who were convarescent. They had been at War- rington, and spoke of it in such golden terms that he was really embarrassed. Coming from London shortly afterwards he met another sol- dier who had been in hospital near 'Warrington, and he also spoke of •the kindness and benefit derived from his Stay in the neighbourhood. Some time ago, at the instance of the Mayoress, Warrington procured an ambulance which was doing good work in France, the Alderman added. Now they had given another for work in the Che,shire district, with the hope that, as the officials of the B.R.C.S. requested, they would be able to send another. It was impossible to do too much-for our soldiers, for they were doing their best for us. Speaking particularly in re- gard to the presence of the Duchess, the Alder- man said he should like to see her more often in Warrington. She not only typified the women of England during this war by her services, but the union of all classes.

In putting the motion to the vote, Miss Sylvia Broadbent said that they could show their practical appreciation at a later date by raising another ambulance or two. Many people

Continued in next column.

Rapid Successes in Shorthand. At a recent examination held under the auspices of the Phonetic Institute, Miss Florence Dawson and Miss Patty Wilding, students of the Warrington Commercial School, Knutsford-road, gained the Advanced Theory Certificate after only eleven weeks' tuition; and Miss Elsie Noneley gained the Elementary Theory Certificate at the end of eight weeks— shorthand being studied as one of the subjects of a course. There were no. failures at the examination.

Pauperisation of Mentally Afflicted Soldiers.

The Board of Guardians, on Wednesday, heartily supported a resolution from the 'Ply- mouth Board of Guardians protesting that soldiers who had ,been mentally afflicted by the war should not be classified as pauper lunatics. The. Chairman (Mr. A. J. Jones) said it was not from any sordid reasons that they sup- ported the resolution in order to reduce the local rates.—The Clerk (Mr. A. Bottomley) mentioned that since the war began three cases had passed through his hands.

Advantages of the School of Art. The prospectus of the Municipal School of Art, Museum-street, for the 1916-17 session has just been issued. It contains the fullest parti- culars regarding the various classes and the fees payable in respect to each subject or craft course, or for single subjects. The scope of training afforded to ambitious students covers as wide and useful an area as that offered by any other similar institution in the kingdom, while the number of distinctions offered for exceptional talent should incite many to ven- ture and win. The teaching staff, with Mr. Clarence Mawson, A.R.C.A. (Lond.) as head- master, are' experts in their different branches of study, and it only requires attention and assiduity, on the pupils' part to secure succesS., A course of instruction in drawing adapted to the needs of teachers in elementary schools has become almost imperative in modern methods of education. It may be availed of in the School of Art.

Wycliffe P.S.A.

_ There was a good meeting on Sunday, Pre- sided over by Mr. B. Farrar, who made refer- ence to that, the 43rd, anniversary of the present buildings of Wycliffe Church, with its pleasing and helpful memories. The .Chair- man welcomed two new Members. The Rev. T. T. James gave the address upon "Be strong." He said he hoped for an increase in their numbers and supported the call of the Chair- man for an increased effort in the future. As the physical and spiritual strength were linked together, he said, one depended upon the other, and their duty was to see to the bodily strength, strength of mind, soul and character. Physical conditions improved by close atten- tion; so spiritual Strength and character could be improved by a hearty co-operation with God's spirit. Mr. W. Warburton rendered "The Lord is my light" and "God shall wipe away all tears." Mr. W. H. Payton presided at the organ. Mr. Beswiek also took part in the meeting. The hymn "Blessed assurance" brought a happy meeting to a close.

Cheshire Teachers Ask for War Bonus.

The elementary ‘school teachers employed in the Runcorn Rural District held a meeting at Frodsham last Saturday, /when it was decided to ask the Cheshire County Education Authority to grant a war bonus according to a scale`approved at that taeeting. It was pointed out that the present purchasing value of £1 was about 12s. 6d., and that such depreciation rendered the present scale of salaries quite inadequte.

Lord Kitchener's Letter to the Nation- \ Those'desirous of seeing the actual autograph letter of Lord Kitchener to the nation and Herkomer's fine portrait ,of the great war leader should visit the Town Hall to-day, when both will be on view. The exhibition will be opened at 11 a.m. by His Worship the Mayor, ana prices of admission are very low. Proceeds 1-11 aid of County Branch British Red Cross Society and Headquarters of the Joint War' Committee. Taxi Collides With Tramcar.

While a taxi-cab behinging to the Patten Arins Hotel, Warrington, was being driven along Liverpool-road, Sankey Bridges, at about 9.55 on Saturday night, it collided with a stationary' tramcar, which, owing to an electric fuse trouble, was not illuminated. The driver of the taxi-cab, Samuel Fidler, residing at 35, HoYle- street, Bewsey, sustained injuries to the stomach and was removed to the Infirmary, from which institution he was discharged on Wednes- day morning. The passenger, who was being driven to Farnworth, escaped unhurt.

Serious Accident to Mr. C. 11. Hibbert. While proceeding hothe for dinner on his bicycle on Thursday, Mr. Charles H. Hibbert, manager of the Weaving Department of Cock" hedge Mills, met with a serious accident. had just passed the foot of Orford-street, who; he came in contact with a motor lorry, aria falling to the ground, his right arm was badlY crushed. He proceeded to Dr. Fox's nursing home in Rylands-street, where it was fauna necessary to amputate the limb. He is novr making satisfactory progress. To Mr. Hibbert, who is well-known in the town and district,. much sympathy will be extended. - Leather Famine. The commandeering by the Government oi practically all the sole leather that is being produced will have far-reaching effects so far ao the general Sublic is concerned, says the War- rington correspondent of the "Leather Trades Review," and, incidentally, it is one of those things which brings the question of the vrar more forcibly into view. ' Although food commodities and such-like have advanced in prices from time to time they have been more or less procurable, but when people find that they cannot get new footweark and repairs done as formerly (and under Pre- sent conditions it would not take long to bring about a famine in boots and shoes), the position is brought home very keenly. There are most things in which people can economise, but with leather being an absolute daily necessity verY little can be done in this respect, and when the matter is looked at from all sides it is to be hoped that it will not be allowed to go to extremes.

OUR FALLEN AND MISSING HEROES.

Further Losses Among Local Men in Various Regiments.

KILLED.

SERGEANT G. ACTON.

Man With Seven Years' Service Killed Instantly.

Through the medium of a letter from fellow soldier, •Mr. and Mrs. J. Acton, 51a, Pierpoint-street, learned that their son, Sergt. George Acton, of the South Lancs. Regiment, had been killed on the 28th August. The letter, dated August 29th, ran as follows:—" Your dear son George was, unfortunately, killed by a German shell yesterday. He was killed in- stantly, so, of course, the poor boy did not sutler. Re died fighting like the good soldier he always was, he was a hero. If it is any consolation to You, you may rest assured he was beloved by all who knew him, and was held in high esteem by Officers anal men of his company. May God help You to bear the terrible loss of one who died for his country and the cause of freedom. Please accept the deepest sympathy of all the and men of the hattlion who knew George, also of one who has lost a true aud brave friend."

° Sergeant Acton, who was 23 years of age, had been in the Army seven years and at the Frpnt from the beginning of the war, coming throtigh unscathed. As a boy he attended Heathside School, and for a-time was employed at the Latchford Tube Works. A keen footballer and

Killed

boxer, he took part in several of. the boxing contests at Orford Barracks while 'stationed there. He played football for his regiment in France, and had only been out of hospital a fortnight—after recovering from a kick in the stomach received when playing football—when he was killed.

DIED OF WOUNDS.

RIFLEMAN J. PARSONAGE.

Lay For Twelve Hours With

Wounded Officer.

Rifleman James Parsonage, a member of the Rifle Brigade, and son of Mr. and Mrs. James Parsonage, 4, Beatrice-street, Latchford, has died of wounds received on August 17tn. The following letter, written to Mrs. Parsonage by a hospital nurse, says :—" He has been very seriously wounded and has lost his right leg. We are doing all we can for him,, and he is having every care and attention. I will write and let you know 'his condition. lam sorry to have to write this bad news, and hope it will net come as ki terrible shock." But the nurse's hopes proved in vain, for by the next post his parents received the fatal news that their son had succumbed to his wounds. Rifleman Parsonage had been wounded and gassed in France before receiving his mortal wounds. When -wounded the first time he lay for twelve hours with a wounded officer, whom, although he told Rifleman Parsonage to leave him, he eventually dragged into safety. Un- fortunately, the officer died of his wounds. James was gassed after returning to the Front in' March of this year. Previous to joining the Army, Rifleman

RIFLEMAN JAMES PARSONAGE.

Died of Wounds,

Parsonage was employed by the St. Helens Cable and Rubber Co., Ltd., attended Christ Church, Latchford, and was an old boy of the Christ Church School. He was very fond ,of football, playing for the Latchford Recreation Football Club, and well-known in the district. He was 26 years of age.

INTERNED TWO YEARS.

Mr. Geo. Monks As A Cricketer

in Germany.

Their son George having spent three years in Germany—two of which have been behind the netting at Ruhleben Camp—Mr. and Mrs. W. 'Monks, of 266, Lovely-lane,' have often wondered how he was looking and it was, there- fore, particularly gratifying that they should receive from him a , portrait the other day. Here it is—one of a group of civilian prisoners in cricket jerseys. "Just as he was," is the parents' observa- tion. Mr. Monks was never very portly, and we cannot expect the Germans to encourage him in that 'direction. Like the others on the photograph, however, he, looks healthy and cheerful, as though he enjoys the good old game

MR. GEO. MONk'CS. Prisoner in Germany

of cricket, of which he used to ,be so fond when at home.

For a number of years Mr. Monks was employed in the office of Messrs. Joseph Cros- field and Sons, Ltd., but, having-a bent towards the study of languages and an ambition to be- come a foreign correspondent, about three years ago he decided to take up his residence in Ger- many with a view to perfecting hij knowledge of and facility in the use of the language of that country. It was his desire that when he bqcame proficient he should return to England and estcblish himself in a commercial office where he could exercise hi linguistic talents in the pursuit of trade. The declaration of war, however, tqrpedoed his plan 'for the time being, for on the 4th August, 1914, the German military arrested him while he was seated at his desk in the office of a Hamburg firm, and in November of 'the same year consigned him to the notorious Ruhleben Camp, where he has remained. Twice a month he sends letttirs home. _ .

Although he ' has to remain passive in the presence of the enemy, Mr. Monks has two brothers who are fighting for their country honour and his release—Lieutenant William Monks and Private Fred Monks. •

Haddon Court Hospital Doctor for Active Service.

Dr. A. J. Hutton having joined the R.A.11.C. for service at the Front, has been obliged to give 'up his duties at Raddon Court Hospital as medical officer in charge, conjointly with Dr. Bower. He has given devoted service to the patients there for nearly two years, and those at present in hospital gave him a hearty send- off early this week, showing their gratitude to him. The staff made him a presentation as a small token of much appreciation. He' carries with him the best wishes of all in his noble work. Dr. Ed. Fox has most kindly come for- ward to fill his place at Raddon Court.

MISSING.

PRIVATE A. J. SHAW.

Last Seen Taking His Gun Back

To Our Trenches.

Private Alexander James Shaw, a Lewis machine gunner in the South Lancs. Regiment, who, before enlisting, lived with his parents at 42, Cumberland-street, Latchford, is officially reported missing since an engagement on the Bth August. Mrs. Shaw wrote to the officer of her son's company inquiring for particulars, and, has received the following reply :—" I very much regret that I cannot give you any news of your son Alec beyond the fact that he has been officially reported missing. As I have only just returned from sick leave in t ngland I was not with the battalion when the action was fought. I have, however, questioned the man who was with your son. Apparently they were both just outside the German barbed wire and were getting their gun back to Our trench when a shell blew the other man into a shell hole, from which he saw your son going on with the machine gun. That was the last that was seen of him. There is a very small chance that he was taken prisoner, but I am afraid it is a very small one. sympathise with you in your great worry and grief, and wish I could give you

PRIVATE A. J. SHAW.

Missing

lowing notice:—" The term missing does not necessarily mean that the soldier is killed or wounded. He may be an unwounded prisoner or temporarily separated fronl his regiment." Mrs. Pepper is naturally much concerned as to the fate of her husband, and would be glad of any news concerning him. Private Pepper, who is twenty-five years of age, has been a member of the Swimming Club for about ten years, and for some tima occupied the position of assistant secretary. An excep- tionally good water polo player, he was captain of the team, for which he has gained the vic- tory many times with his favourite back-hand shots. Ile has won many prizes in swimming contests. As a boy he attended the National Schbol, qurch-street, and before enlisting was employed in the drapery department of the Co- operative Society, Sankey-street. He was a member, of St. Paul's Church,, and treasurer for the Bible Claks. He joined the Army in January of this year, and had been .at the Front exactly two months to the day he was-reported missing. His brother, William, is in the Australian Navy, and jais father is serving with the National Reserve.

Garden Party at Tower House.

At the invitation of Colonel G. R. Crosfield, D. 5.0., a garden party was given at Tower House, Bewsey, on Tuesday afternoon to the women who meet occasionally at the Drill Hall to provide comforts for the men of the South Lancashire Regiment. Mr. J. H. Smethurst, Miss Jackson, the housekeeper, and Mr. Town- ley, head gardener, looked after the wants of the visitors, who numbered about sixty. The weather was beautifully fine, andl a most enjoy- able evening was spent. There was a ladies' bowling main, which was eagerly contested, the winners being: 1, Mrs. Tyrnan; 2, Mrs. Antrobus; 3, Mrs. Tryner. Tea was served on the lawn.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0086.txt | included=true | included =====
ST r lES OF THE WAR.

TRYING TIMES IN AFRICA,

Lions And Bees Added To Our Him Enemies.

A generous subscriber, to 'Mr. W. Stewart Royston's walking stick fund, sends a long and very interesting letter from a brother, who is motor transport driver with the British Ex— peditionary Force in German East Africa: Driver Archie Waddie writes:—

"We have been very busy, and have had ft pretty rough for the last six weeks. lam with an ammunition column attached to a South !African infantry division, and we have been chasing the Germans for about 200 miles in their own territory. I am not going to describe everything that has happened during that time, as it would fill a volume, but suffice`it to 'say we have had a hard time, what with sickness, insufficient food, and want of water. About three weeks ago, when we were coming into camp cone night, the Germans shelled our con- voy with a\big gun and knocked out two chaps. rho Lext night my lurry and another were told cif to go back fourteen miles to fetch supplies, and that right, as it was late and (lark, we decided to camp out on the veldt, but had to shift in a hurry on account of lions. It was an experience Z don't wish to go through again. About three nights agO, as we came into camp we heard a commotion going .on, machine gun and 'rifle fire, and we were called up at 5 a.m. to stand by with our rifles, but it blew past, as the 5.A.1., who were ahead, managed to scatter the Germans. The next day we passed 17 graves of our fellows who had gone under, and later on came to a big hole, loosely filled in. On going up to see what it was, we found it contained some dead Geman Askaris. They mast have buried diem in a hurry, as one man's shoulder protruded from the sand. Yesterday we were held up by the mule trans- port in front, and heard that they were held op by snipers, and one Indian was shot. We sent out Pickets into the bush, but they returned in about half an hour's time, having seen nothing, so we proceeded, but were held UP again, this time it was the Germans shelling us. They had got the range of the road along which we were travelling, and were letting us have some with their big guns. Luckily, all their. shots went wide, and we got in all right. They continued shelling us in camp for about an hour.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0087.txt | included=true | included =====
Gave the Germans a " Pasting."

"To-day has been, up to the present, quite quiet. A certain Indian regiment did well here yesterday, and,. I believe, has given the Germans a good pasting, capturing some prisoners and machine guns. Yesterday we came through a village hurriedly evacuated by the Germans. They left the remains of a Ford car, dozens of gallons- of paraffin and oil—but, the artful beggars, each tin had been punctured with a pick-axe—and some dead niggers. One of them must have been shot by the Germans them- selves, as his hands were bound to his sides with a leather belt, and he was shot in the back of the head. I have not, seen bread for six weeks, fresh meat three tittles during that time. We have been living on mealy meal. It makes good porridge. By- the way, I am a bit of a baker now, and have been baking scones this afternoon. I have been terribly hard put for tobacco, and was reduced to smoking dried tea leaves, but bought some native stick tobacco off some German niggers, but could not smoke it, as it is even stronger than the thick twist the navvies smoke at home.

"At the present moment we are nearly eaten up with flies. A week or so ago -a thing hap- pened which. you might hardly credit. A mule transport somehow or other disturbed a bees' hive, and the bees swarmed round in thousands, stung three mares to death, and left the driver in a ,sorry plight, the bees actually getting in- side his shirt. / have just been talking to a wounded South African, who passed through on his way to hospital. Poor fellow,. his brother waA killed in the same action."

===== 0002601_19160909_art0088.txt | included=true | included =====
BLINDED ' FOR YOU!

Blind Musicians To Give A Concert In Parr Hall.

The most pathetic cases that the war has produced are those which are rapidly making a little army of men who have lost their sight as the result of wounds, and no effort should be spared to make their lot as happy and free from tmneussary care as it is possible to make it. I ,

We welcome the announcement that a concert will be given in Parr Hall next Friday week, September 22nd, by "The Blind Musicians," in aid of the soldiers and sailors who have been blinded in, the war. As is now fairly widely known, a hostel has been established at St. Dunstan's, Regent's Park, London, kindly lent by Mr. Otto Kahn. It is a fine house with ex- cellent facilities for recreation in the grounds, and this hostel is maintained by The National Institute for the Blind, the British Red Cross --- Society, and die Order of St. John of Jerusa- lem, and is ma4Saged by the Blinded Soldiers' and Sailors' Care Committee. By arrangement with the War Office all blinded soldiers upon discharge from the military hospitals are in- vited to enter St. Dunstan's, where "they may be taught to be blind." Everything that can be done has been done for the comfort and training of these young men who have been suddenly plunged into lifelong darkness; and every effort Is being made by those interested to lighten the existence of the sufferers and to face , cheerfully their changed surroundings with every encouragement. An effort of this charac- ter needs no recommendation to the sympathies of the public; it commends itself. The concert in the Parr Hall will doubtless receive, as it ought to receive, the most liberal support.

LOCAL FACTS AND FANCIES.

By CRITIC.

There was something doing at Lymm last Saturday. Everyone' seemed to be out for the day.

How is it that the wounded soldiers from Brookqeld did not take part in the procession?

The village presented a pretty spectacle, being literally one great mass flags and —hunting.

The platform on which the presentation was made was no doubt the prettiest of "all, and the Council employes are to be congratulated on their efforts.

Next came the approach to the hero's resi- dence at Newfields. The entire row of houses displayed flags from their windows, and over- head hung great streamers.

His own home was no less beautiful. The frontage was decorated with flags and bunting, and hanging just over the door was a card with the following inscription "Honour to whom honour is due."

i ..,i;'.. :1 i

"He deserved it." So say we all.

Celebrations of a private nature were held on Saturday night.

Who were the two men who had to be rebuked for not removing their hats when the band played the National Anthem?

I ' I only hope a few critics I heard a while ago ..! heard what Field's officers had to say 'of him • last Saturday.

A local soldier writing from France says, "Have you got any motor boats on the lake yet?"

Perhaps he will understand when he hears about the "Bear Garden'" part of the business.

And it will be a consolation for him to know that there is still one on the bank.

What a time we had at Beechwood last Saturday, and what glorious weather!

Everyone seemed to be out for fun, and every- one seemed to find plenty.

The concert hall was exceedingly popular, for at both performances would-be patrons had to be turned away.

ct '

The "Queries in Querieland " were in great demand, for we had not forgotten their splendid performance last winter.

One of their striking features is "That no , male member of the company is eligible for the 1 ' Army."

By the way, since the last concert they gave here, Mr. George Stubbs and Miss Dorothy L. Burgess have become Mr. and Mrs. Geo. Stubbs.

Ex-Gunner James Plant (the manager) has in his company :something to oe proud of, for never have concerts been given in the village with better effect than those rendered by his admirable company.

What about the storm last Sunday? It did rain a little, didn't it,

Persistency Rewarded.—Mr. Horace Hudson, The Nook, Booth's Hill, has applied for enlist- ment and been ## ically examined and rejected no fewer than f # urteen times. But under the new attestation scheme he has been accepted for general service at a Warrington recruiting centre. He is expecting to "join up" as soon as the new Bill comes into operation at the end of this month.

Prisoner Of War Sends For Suit Of Khaki.— A letter received ,this week by his parents at Rush Green from Private Walter Massey, the Cheshire Regiment, who has been a prisoner of war in "Germany since the memorable retreat from Mons, states that they are doing very well, and are receiving pretty decent food, but he would be Very much obliged if they would send him a suit of khaki as early as possible as his own is worn out.

RED CROSS HOSPITAL.

Countess Of Stamford Opens Working Men's Garden Fete.

On Saturday afternoon and eveninc, a most attractive and successful garden fete, organ- ised by the Lymm Detachment Working Men's Committee : in aid of the British Red Cross Society Hospital, was held (by kind 'per- mission of Sir W. H. Lever, Bart.) in the charming residence' and. grounds of Beechwood, , The magnificent weather which favoured the presentation of the D.C.M. to Sergeant Field earlier in the day remained unbroken, with the result that the fete took place under ideal conditions. In case of adverse climatic condi- tions all the amusements, concerts, &c., would have been held under cover. As it was the concerts were held in the house and spaciolis apartments were attractively laid out as re- freshment cafes. All the arrangements were extremely creditable to the Working Men's Committee, who had as their chairthan Mr. K. Tweedale; as hon. secretary, Mr. H. Halli- day; and as lion. treasurer and übiquitous organiser of the fete, Mr. A. H. Hudson.. •

The Band of- the South Lancashire Regiment was a great help and attraction, their pro- 'gramme of selections being exceedingly bright and pleasing. Two enjoyable concerts were given by "The Queries in Querieland," a clever party of vocalists who aptly described themselves as a "Queer Medley of Queries." Then there were the usual numerous attrac- tions of a fete of this nature and a whist drive was held in -the evening. Quite a number of wounded soldiers were present during the day' and frolicked in the sunshine as well as they could.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0094.txt | included=true | included =====
A Working Vice-President.

There was a large company present when the Countess of Stamford declared the fete open. Mr. A. de C. Parmiter, who is a member of the General. Committee, said .his duty that afternoon was ec very pleasant one to ask the Countess of Stamford, whose presence that afternoon they so much appreciated, to declare the garden fete open. Lady Stamford was vice-president of the British Red Cross Society for that district in Cheshire. He: did not want them to be under any misapprehension as to what vice-president meant. He supposed some of them' had been. vice-presidents of guilds and institutions Or things of ,the-kind' and found that they had nothing at all to do with the management and that they were not expected to do anything except to send a contribution to the funds once a year. (Laughter.) But a vice-president of the Red Cross meant a very different thing. It involved a great deal of very real and responsible work.. They in Lymm were very lucky to be presided over by Lady Stamford, because when they had to appeal to her for -help anti guidance it had always been given willingly and proved of the greatest assistance.' (Applause.) With reference to the Working 'Men's Committee he might say that during the whole time this hospital had been opened their work had been simply splen- did. They had worked steadily and persistently and-had been the means of handing over to the hospital several hundred pounds towards. its funds. The carrying on of the hospital. there had been of absolute importance. Then they had the fact that the Working Men's Committee had entirely organised 'and 'got up that 'fete. (Applause.) .The hospital at the present time was practically full and had been for some weeks, and, as far as they could sew was likely to be for some time to come, so that they would want every penny they could get. _ _ .

===== 0002601_19160909_art0095.txt | included=true | included =====
Lady Stamford Congratulates Lymm.

Lady Stamford expressed the great pleasure it gave her to do something to help Lymm. Lymm had been very strenuous in doing such hard work: it had done all it could for the hospital. She should refer for one minute to what had just been said about her. In the Altrincham district people had been so effi- cient, so energetic and determined, that every hospital when it was started had run itself, and generally the wheels had been very well oiled., (Applause.) She very heartily congra- tulated the Working Men's Committee upon the work they had done. It was always rather a difficult thing to go on collecting subscrip- tions and getting donations for a work which had been going on like this for two years. But those who set themselves deliberately to do what was wanted had been more than re- paid already. In years to come what a very fine reputation they would have, as the people who stepped into the gap and did what was wanted. She congratulated them upon having taken that pleasant garden and she had great pleasure in declaring the fete open and wish- ing it very great- success. (Appleuse.) Mr. Alfred Watkin said he was not himself officially connected with the Red Cross Hospi- tal at Lymm, but his wife, as township leader, was, and as her spokesman he asked them to accord a hearty vote of thanks to Lady Stam- ford for, coming there and speaking to them in such a kind and eloquent way. • • A cheer was given for Lady Stamford, the band played "God save the King," and the fun of the fair began. •

===== 0002601_19160909_art0096.txt | included=true | included =====

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 9, 1916.

"WELCOME !"

Gallipoli Hero Enthusiastically

Greeted And Decorated.

A FIELD DAY.

Beneath a scorching sun, which must have reminded the daring soldier of the consuming heat of the Gallipoli Peninsula or of his pre- vious campaign on .the South African veldt, Thomas W. Field (late Lancashire Fusiliers) was last Saturday proudly decorated in his native village of Lymm with the medal which is only• one remove from the highest award for valour.

Our readers are already aware of the magni- ficent deed which earned the Distinguished Conduct Medal for this dashing non-corn. Like all our heroes, Quartermaster Sergeant Field shunned publicity, but luckily; "some have honours thrust upon them," and he was obliged to -submit to the ordeal of having the medal pinned on his breast by the gallant Colonel of his regiment, of listening to the praises of brave Vajor Alexander, who led his batta- lion into action, and of seeing the delight and enthusiasm of his fellow-townsmen.

A platform, gaily festooned with streamers, and on which was a table covered with the Union Jack,• had' been erected in front of the strangely interesting old-time Cross, and here the ceremony was performed. Nearly every house in the vicinity displayed a flag of large dimensions, and the mottoes " Welcome " and "Honour to whom honour is due" were ap- propriately prominent. The spacious square was packed with people and there was great enthusiasm. Shortly before two o'clock a pro- cession was formed at the Council Offices. In front came a mounted detachment of the Cheshire Volunteers, followed by the fine Band of the South Lancashire Regiment, playing lively airs. • Then marched a contingent of the Volunteer Training Corps, and the members of the Lyrana Urban District Council. In a motor car, bright with miniature flags and decorated with flowers, was seated the hero of the day, and beside him Lieutenant- Colonel Alan McClure, in command of the

hats, handkerchiefs and miniature flags, Colonel McClure pinned the medal on Sergeant Field's breast, the band playing the National Anthem. Quartermaster-Sergeant Field expressed his deep gratitude for the reception they had given him and expressed the hope that when the men came home they would get the right royal welcome they deserved. Mr. J. H. Holt, vice-chairman of the Urban Council, moved a vote of thanks to Lieutenant- Colonel McClure for coming to present the medal, and the honour he had bestowed upon the village. - It. had occurred to him when listening to the description of -the acts of bravery carried out by Quartermaster-Sergeant Field that the keynote which had been struck was that of tenacity. He felt that what was going to win this war for us was British tenacity. Mr. Holt also included the other officers present in the vote of thanks. There was a vigorous response. Colonel McClure said the vote of thanks was quite unnecessary. Any officer would be glad to do what he could for any mew in the battalion. They were always good friends and would remain good friends to the end. . When they saw any chanee of recommending any good work done by the men of the battalion they were only too glad to do so. (Cheers.) Those present then sang "God Save the King" with much heartiness, and- cheers hav- ing- been given for Quartermaster-Sergeant -and Mrs. Field, the procession was re-formed and headed by the band and greatly augmented in numbers, moved off to Beechwood, where the garden fete in aid of the Red Cross Hospital was about to be opened.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0101.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE ERNEST JONES.

AVound AVhich Means The Loss Of A Finger.

Private Ernest Jones, Warwickshire Regi- ment, has been wounded in the left hand whilst fighting in Prance. Writing to his parents at Sandy-lane from a base hospital, he states that he had lost one of the middle fingers of the left hand. This was closely followed by an official letter from the War Office confirm- ing the gallant soldier's statement. Soon after the outbreak of war he enlisted in

fighting in France. It is just the sort that an Englishman likes. I bet the Turks thought so, for' the way in which we handled them and re- pulsed their attack; well, it must have been absolute slaughter. We have marched and ad- vanced miles. In my own opinion, this part of of the business is the most trying of all, owing to the great lack of water. We have nothing to depend upon for it but the camels and wells. We were 36 hours without a bite of anything but unripe dates, but thank God we are doing a little better now, for five biscuits and a tin of bully beef a day is something to be thankful for. You must not think that I am down-hearted, for I am not, for if I was I don't think that I should have been alive to-day. It is a little disheartening when you are march- ing across the dAert with nothing around you only sand, and your comrades are continually falling out, but what a relief when you spot a clump of palm trees, and although it takes hours to reach them, it seems to give you new hope. Unfortunately the dates are not yet ripe, but water is the thing that matters, and where there are palm trees you can always depend on find- ing water if you dig deep enough. We are at present resting under a tree at the corner of a wood, and I can tell you we had quite a scrap to get this position, though I have no idea where we are. I was very sorry to learn through 'The Examiner that Lymm has again lost two of its most popular and respected men, 'Harry Brookes and Tom Read, for they were truly two of the best, and I hope that their relatives will accept my deepest sympathy." . . _ _ .

_ Private James Jennings has written numer- ous letters that have appeared in our columns, and he is looked upon by "Examiner" *readers as a gifted correspondent.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0102.txt | included=true | included =====
PRIVATE HAROLD AVIBBERLEV.

Gassed And Buried In trench By A Shell Explosion. Vrivate Harold Wibberley, of the Cheshire Regiment, a popular Statham soldier, has .been gassed and buried in a trench. Writing to some relatives in 'Statham from the 3rd Cana- dian Stationary Hospital, the gallant soldier says :

"I am mending nicely, so do not worry.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0103.txt | included=true | included =====
QR.-M.-SERGI'. FIELD. D.C.M.

regiment, whio , had 'been wounded. Major Alexander, D. 5.0., who led the attack in which Quartermaster-Sergeant Field afterwards gained the distinction; Mr. F. Isockyer (chairman of Lymm Urban District Council) and Mr. W. Mullard (clerk to that .body) Were in the same vehicle. In another 'motor car were Captain Cartwright (officer commanding the depot of the regiment) and Captain Gledhill: Mrs. Field and her little daughter were also present and came in for many congratulations. , On reaching the Cross the occupants of the motor cars took their seats on the platform, and the proceedings were opened without delay by Mr. Lockyer, who presided. How the Village has Helped the Nation.

Mr. Lockyer said they were met on that occa- sion to show their deep appreciation of, and gratitude to, one of their fellow townsmen, ex- Q.11.-Sergt. Field, for the gallant service which he rendered to his King and country in the terrible struggle which was-now taking place in Europe. Having described the feat performed by Q.M.-Sergt. Field, and stated that, in addition to earning this medal, he was mentioned in dis- patches on the 12th December, 1915, Mr. Lockyer said it did not require any words of his to be added to that simple story of British gallantry and courage to enable them to realise what such service meant. if it had not been for such men as Q.M.-Sergt. Field those present would not be where they were that day, and he sincerely hoped that they would have an opportunity of showing their, gratitude to him in a substantial manner in the very near future. He (Mr. Lockyer) could not help thinking of the gallant fellows frBm that village who, during that war, had made the great sacrifice and laid down their lives for their country; they now, he believed, numbered seventeen, and the exact nature of their deaths was in some cases not known. Some of them lay in unknown graves in a foreign country, and those at home were left to mourn their loss. The horrors of the war had been specially brought home to him during the last few days-4e had very recently lost his own son- in-law, who had died from wounds. Let them who were left at home see to it that the deeds of the gallant fellows who returned to them, in some cases' sick and wounded, were properly rewarded, and that the memory of those who fell was never allowed to fade. (Cheers.)

How the Distinction was Won.

Colonel McClure said before presenting the medal to Q.M.-Sergt. Field he would ask Major Alexander, who was in command of the battalion on the 7th August, to sneak a few words. • Major Alexander, who was warmly cheered, assured his hearers that he was glad to be pres- ent to say a few words in support of the very good work that Q.M.-Sergt. Field did when he was in the Peninsula. Often an official account is a very bald one, and one does not know as to the exact thing that took place: On this occasion, his (Major Alexander's) battalion and others had to advance and capture Turkish trenches. They got into their trench and the other battalions fell back. Then it was that Sergeant Field first of all got out behind the trench and showed a red flag for the guidance of the artillery to let them know they had got there. When the men on either flank fell back Sergeant Field clung to his position with very few men. He was noticed in that position by Captain Gledhill, who was then adjutant, and he promptly organised an attack and they got back into the trench. He was absolutely convinced that had it not been for the dogged determination and bravery shown by Sergeant Field they should have all had desperately hard wolik to get back into the trench. •He (Major Alexander) was particu- larl, pleased that he was able to give such a favourable • report to the higher authorities, And afterwards to learn that Sergeant Field had got the Distinguished Conduct Medal. "He deserves it," concluded Major Alexander, amidst renewed cheering. Colonel McClure Said he understood that Lymm had got representatives in many regi- ments, and although Sergeant Field was the first to win the D.C.M., it was only one of many that would come there. He understood that in addition to Major Alexander, who had won the D. 5.0., another inhabitant of Lymm was awarded the Russian order—the Cross ef St. George. •

===== 0002601_19160909_art0104.txt | included=true | included =====
The Army Order.

Colonel MdClure thep, read the Army Order, as under:—

" Distinguished Conduct Medal, issued witli Army Orders, Ist April, 1916, No. 2228 Sergt. T. W. Field, 1/7th Lancashire Fusiliers, T.F. For conspicuous gallantry on the 7th August, 1915, on the Gallipoli Peninsula. The Turks having recaptured a portion of a trench, Sergeant Field, with three other man, with great bravery, held on to the centre of the position until a counter attack had been organised, which drove the enemy out. He was wounded in four places, and of the others, two were killed ond one-wounsled." The Medal Presented.

PRIVATE JOHN PERCIVAL.

Son Of n Ex-Army Pensioner Suffering From Shell Shock.

Private John Percival, the Cheshire Regi- ment, is seriously ill suffering-from shell shock. His mother, 16, Oldfield-road, Statham, received the news this week in an official communication sent from the War Office, the .writer stating that in the event of her son's condition becom- ing any graver the news would at once be con- veved to her. •

Private John Percival is an exceedingly well- known local man, and for some considerable time was a popular member of the local Terri- torial Force. At the outbreak of war he was

===== 0002601_19160909_art0106.txt | included=true | included =====
mobilised with this corps, but was rejected by the local .doctor. Not being satisfied he again approached the recruiting authorities and was eventually accepted and placed in. the Cheshire Regiment. After ten Months' training he was drafted out to: France in June, 1916. He is 21 years of age,-and-wass educated at the 'Lymm and, St4tham Schools, and worshipped at the Lymm' Paiish Church. Prior to enlisting he was em- ployed in the locomotive department belonging to the Manchester Ship Cfinal Company at Par- tington. His brother, Private Peter Percival, of the Cheshire Regiment, was wounded about -twelve months ago, and is at present in England. His father, who died about 14 years ago, was an Army pensioner, with over 21 years' regular service to his credit.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0107.txt | included=true | included =====
THE LONG-WAITED ATTACK.

Private James Jenninas And "A Bit 01 A Do " With The Turks.

Writing to his home at Moston-street, New- fields, Private James Jennings, of the Maiiches- ter Regiment, sends an amazing account of an attack with the-Turks.

"You will be surprised," he says, "to heAr that we have had 'a bit of a do' with the Turks, for the other night ,the long-waited attach came at last. They were soon repulsed, for we were all over them before they knew, where they were, and we drove thexa back about 30 miles, and cap- tured thousands of prisoners. , As regards casualties in the fighting, we had very few. It is open warfare here, and not like the trench

think the real cause of my being here so ill is thriiiigh being buried in the' trenches the other night. I can stand the bullets flying about all right, but what kills my ,pig are the shells. They come over 'whizz,' and when they drop they send tons. of earth flying in the air. It is a lively place where our lads are; they tell me that Ypres is nothing near so bad. We are at . I suppose the football season will just be about commencing. I wish I was back, for I am very fond of football. You know I am not fed up, only I have had quite enough. We are having some very wet weather again now, and it makes the trenches very bad. It is not so pleasant, I can assure you, when you have to stand in one place for several hours. Remember me to all the bhoys,' and tell them that after this I hope/ to be in ' Blighty ' for a short time." Private Harold Wibberley is a footballer of some renown. He was educated at the Lymm and Statham Schools and worshipped at the Primitive Methodist Church. He enlisted in March of this year and went out to France about nine weeks ago. Prior to the war he was in the TerritoriarS. In civil life he is a carter and was employed by a Warrington firm. His brother, Private Tom Wibberley, also of the Cheshire Regiment, we reported wounded a few weeks ago.

STOCKTON HEATH.

Evening Services to be Earlier.—ln conse- quence of the lighting regulations, commencing in October the Sunday 'evening service at St. Thomas' Church will be at 3.30 instead of 6.30, and the Sacrament of Holy Baptism will be administered on Fridays at 10.30 a.m. instead of on Sundays at 3 p.m., until further notice.

G. R. WILLIAMSON.

Belgian Refugees, Beechwood House, Lymm.— A, public meeting will be held in the Assembly Room at the' Conservative Club, Lymm, on Wed- nesday evening , next, to receive the hon. treasurer's statement of accounts in connection with the Belgian Refugees at Beechwood House. All 'subscribers invited to attend.

Sergeant Arthur Read Wounded.—Just on going to press we hear that Sergeant Arthur Read, Newfields, of the has been wounded, his mother having received a field card to that effect. Further particulars are not yet to hand. He is a' brother to Private William Read, whom we reported wounded last week.

MEMORIAL SERVICE.

'tribute At St. Thomas' To The Gallant Dead.

St. Thomas' Church, Stockton Heath, was crowded on :Sunday afternoon, Evhen a solemn memorial service was held for those who had been killed in action and drowned at sea. The names of the fallen were read in the course of the service by the Rev. J. Collins (vicar), the Dead March in "Saul" being first played on the organ. They were :—Alfred Allender, Richard Gamble, Frank Foster Isher- wood, Harry Parton, Peter Valentine Plinston, John Henry Sanford, Reginald Joseph Shill- cock, George Taylor and William Vere.

The service opened with the favourite hymn "Now the labourer's task is o'er," followed by the chanting of the special Psalms and he reading of the lesson. The recital of the jtoll of Honour was extremely pathetic and was succeeded by prayers from the'bUrial service and the hymn "The Saints of God! Their conflict past; and life's long battle won at last."

- The Rev. J. R. Ji;nes (vicar of Great Sankey) delivered a brief but extremely sympathetic address of high appreciation for, the fallen and comfort for those who are 19ft behind. He referred to the splendid way in which the young men of the country had come forward. Their spirits would never -die. The touching words were all the more powerful coming from the lips of one who had himself lost a gallant son.

After the concluding hymn and Benediction, Mr. Rose played Beethoven's Funeral March.

PRIVATE G. E. PENNY.

Suffering From Shell Shock; Now In Convalescent Camp.

Mrs. Penny, of 37, Cawdor-street, Stockton Heath„ has received a letter from her son, Private G. ,E. Penny, of the South Lancashire Regiment, stating that he had been in hospital suffering from shell shock. He was at the time of writing well behind the line in a convales- cent camp. He said that he got a nasty shak- ing up and thought that his time had come. Private Penny is a Son of the late Mr. J. Penny, of New Brighton. He is 22 years of age and was formerly employed in the Goods De- partment of the London and North-Western Railway Company, Bank Quay. He has two brothers serving in the -Army.

PAD GATE.

Choir Trip.--On Saturday the annual trip of the choir of Christ Church, Padgate, took' place. The places visited were. Liverpool and New Brighton. At Liverpool the Art Gallery and Museum were visited, and it New Bri4hton the time was spent in paddlim and other amusements. The weather was ideal. The party were accompanied by the Rev. J. Hirst and Mr. F. Blackwell, organist. ,

, Garden Meeting at the V icarage.—A garden meeting in ad of the work of the Church Missionary Society was held in the Vicarage Grounds, Padgate, on Friday, September Ist. Miss Annie Maddison, C.M.S. missionary, Chekiang, China, and the Rev. A. T. Goodrich, organising secretary of the society, were present. Others present included Rev. B. Percival, Dr. Bennett, Messrs. E. Gorton, J. Hamblett, W. Jump, and a -number of ladies. Mrs. Gorton, who acted as hostess, provided tea, and a c6l- lection on behalf of the society realised £7 10s.

Private Ward Wounded Again.—News has been received by Airs. Ward of Cinnamon-lane, Fearnhead, to say that her son, Private Scott Ward, has been severely wounded in the head, and is at present lying in hosuital in France. Some time ago Private Ward was in hospital in England suffering fiora wounds in the arm and leg, which he received in Gallipoli. Before leaving for New Zealand he lived with his parents in Fearnhead, but came over to Eng- land with the Australian contingent, which fought so bravely in Gallipoli.

Tennis Club Entertain Wounded Soldiers.:— The members of the Padgate Tennis and Bowl-

jug Club again extended an invitation to the wounded soldiers from Whitecross Hospital to visit their grounds on: Thursday.- About forty were conveyed in a motor 'bus kindly lent by the Warrington Corporation. On arrival at the grounds each soldier was presented with cigar- ettes. Bowling, tennis and croquet were heartily indulged in. Tea was provided by the lady members, the soldiers doing full justice to the excellent repast. Mr. E. Lunt (presideht of the club) welcomed the wounded men, and Sergeant Rushton •responded, and thanked all the members for their kind hospitality. . A bowling main , resulted :—I,. Private Burgess; 2, Pioneer T. Davies; 3, Private. Stanley; 4, Lance-Corporal Curren. Mr..E.dLunt presented the prizes. An enjoyable impromptu concert was held, several soldiers and nienabers taking part. Mr. P. H. Bennett very kindly supplied the men with fruit. The arrangements were carried out by Messrs. R. Tunstall and' R. W. Cook.

Church Lade Brigade.—The Padgate C.L.B- spent a very enjoyable week-end camp on the Vicarage lawn. On Sunday afternoon they were inspected by Lieutenant-Colonel J. D. Buckton, V.D. (of North Nibley, Gres.). Commander Frost (of the Volunteer Training Corps), Major Brookes and Major Ford (staff officers of the Warrington Cadet Battalion), the Rev. 3- Hirst, and the company's officers (Captain Simpson and Lieutenant Peters). C'okmel Buekton inspected the ranks, arms and equip- ment, and afterwards spoke to the boys, re- viewing the work in its religious aspect. He also spoke of the honour the uniform conveyed as it was identical with that worn by our brave fellows in the field. Members must be careful never to bring disgrace on it. Colonel Buckton claimed to be the originator of the original C.L.B. which flourished in Padgate many years ago, under the late Major Appleton. The weather was very unsettled all day on Sunday, but fortunately kept fine for the inspection. At the conclusion the officers were entertained to tea in the Vicarage by the Vicar (the Rev. 3. Hirst).

ORFORD.

VICAR'S TRIBUTE TO DEAD'

SOLDIERS.

Writing in this month's "Parish Magazine" the Vicar of Orford states:— "Two more names are this month transferred to the list of those Within the Veil. On July 24th Gunner Oliver Livesey Booth was killed instantaneously by direct shell fire. lie was 41 years of age and was among the first batch of attendants at Winviick Asylum to volunteer for active service abroad. His qualities of never failing cheerfulness and indomitable bravery had endeared him to his comrades at the Front, by whom his loss is sorely felt. I believe I ara right in saying that at the time of his death his name had been recommended for distinction for an act of bravery performed a short time pre- viously:

"Private Wm. Parsonage was only 17 years old at the time of his death, which occurren, on August 3rd from wounds in the chest re' ceived twp days before. He was a member of the Boys" Bible Class and was confirmed three years ago. He was an example of that youthful valour which has brightened the scroll of Eng- lish fair fame in this war—a' young lad with the courage of a man and willing to do a man's. work on behalf of a great cause and to take a man's chances. Both of these we shall still remem- ber mn. our daily prayers, and shall include their bereaved ones in our petitions for those Who are .left behind sorrowful, and yet with the com- fort of knowing that with them all will be well, for their sacrifice was made cheerfully and in a righteous cause."

PICTURISED ROMANTIC NOVEL BY ROY L.McCAIZIELL ADAPTED FROM THE SENSATIONAL —FLYING .A:e SERIAL FILM BY ALEC J. BRAID.

riders in this story are purely imaginary living

SYNOPSIS OF PRECEDING CHAPTERS

Colonel Arthur Stanleigh and Judge Lamar Stan- leigh, cousins, of Virginia. are bitter enemies over the Possession of The Diamond from the Sky, a massive jewel heirloom found centuries ago in a fallen meteor. Colonel Stanleigh, when a daughter is born, buys the male heir of a gipsy king, that he may keep the jewel. He plans to rear Esther, the girl, in secrecy. Esther and The Diamond from the Sky are stolen by Hagar. the gipsy mother. Years later Hagar returns Esther and the diamond, but of the old group only Dr. Lee is living. Blair Stanleigh kills Dr. Lee and steals the jewel. Blair Stanleigh and Arthur Stanleigh, the false heir, fight a duel. Arthur, thinking he has killed his cousin, flees in a racing motor car. In his mad fight the machine goes over a river bank, and that night the moon shines on a body floating down the stream. . Arthur is found by Esther and Hagar and nursed to health. Hagar tells him his real identity. Arthur swears to make himself worthy of Esther, and leaves taking the diamond, and meets Blair Stanleigh. Blair takes him to a great ball, where they meet Vivian Marston, a New York belle, wearing the diamond. Hagar is there, too, telling fortunes. Vivian has just seated herself when a brawny fore- arm clutches The Diamond from the Sky. ' A greater confusion than that caused by the loss of the diamond arises when the Sheriff of Fairfax ar- rives at the ball and demands the arrest of Arthur Stanleigh. He escapes, partly through the efforts of Hagar, and later finds work and refuge as a farm hand. Luke Lovell drops the diamond into a mail bag. The, gem clings by its clasp to a rent in the bag, and is afterwards found by Quabba, the hunch- back organ-grinder. He does not retain it long, Clarence, his monkey, drops "it in an owl's nest. Arthur, working on a farm as "John Powell," masks himself and goes to the Fairfax tournament, where he wins the highest honour. He is betrayed by Blair Stanleigh, and amid great confusion the young Virginians fight to bar Arthur's escape without avail. Hagar, holding proofs of Blair Stanleigh's guilt, is brutally attacked by the young man, and her mind destroyed. The diamond finds its way to a Chinese opium den, whence Blair steals it. Vivian marries him, and they escape from Tom Blake in a train, which robbers board and take the diamond -- with other jewels and money. Pursued by a- Sheriff's posse, the train robbers lose the diamond and treasure, the latter being found by "John Powell." Later, an Indian squaw finds the diamond, which Luke Lovell purchases, only to lose it through the treachery of a partner. Quabba is injured in an accident, and Blair, by a false message, prevails upon Esther to visit the wounded man. He meets her, and demands the Stanleigh document. The girl defies him, and is attacked. Tom Blake comes to the rescue. "John Powell" has purchased some mines, and Esther, with Marmaduke, go to the mountains in search" of him. -They complete the journey in a coach. A linch-pin breaks, and Luke, now employed by a blacksmith, pretends to, fit a new pin, but, at the instigation of Blair, replaces the broken pin, and the coach falls over a cliff and is smashed. Quabba, finds and loses the diamond.

CHAPTER XV

,

and have no connection with or relation to any person.]

dress-suit case that had fallen from the crushed and shattered coach.

In this way Esther was borne to the blacksmith's shop. She knew the document was safe from those evil hands under the rock by the coach and, bad as she knew Luke Lovell was, she felt that no great harm would befall her while he was by. But the men were no nearer the secret than before. The document they sought, was not in the suit case they brought from the wreckage. Instead the case contained Marmaduke's belongings, and, cajole or threaten, they could not gain any informa- tion from the girl. Then they tied her to the anvil and locked the shop while they went back to the coach for the case near the dead lawyer. Marma- duke, however, was uninjured, and muttering, "The young lady passenger has gone, and I am jolly well going myself," the bruised man of the law had limped away. _

Alone in the- blacksmith's shop with night fall- ing, Esther summoned her strength to free the bonds. Daring, resourceful Stanleigh blood flowed in the girl's veins, and the terrifying situation did not appal her. Pressing the knotted rope upon a piece of iron still hot , from the forge she held it firmly until the strands gave way. and she was free. Frantically she smote the door, calling for help. S_ _

_ Quabba, mourning his young mistress and be- wailing his fate that he had so strangely found and lost the diamond again, had set out on foot for the mines to find Arthur, but especially to find Esther, his young mistress. It was just at nightfall that he reached the forge, some three miles yet from the mines. It was locked, gloomy, and deserted in the darkness. But from within he heard a voice he recognised—ihe voice of his young mistress calling for help. A sledge-hammer stood by the door. Quabba, seized it and shattered down the door, and soon he was shedding tears of joy as he clasped the hands of Esther. There was no time for explanation now. A look from Esther, and the two sped off through the dark- ness, to be followed a few minutes later by Blair and Luke, returning to find their fair prisoner had been freed and was gone. Over the mountains through the darkness went pursuers and pursued.

_ Dior broke on the other side of the mouqain at the Lady Veronica mines. The hooter at the power- house woke the echoes of the mountain gOrge, rous- ing the sleeping miners. The whistle woke Esther and Quabba where they had slept on the rugged mountain-side a mile away, and it also roused to their evil purposes Blair Stanleigh and Luke Lovell. "That is the mine whistle!" exclaimed Quabba. "If we can reach the mine we shall find Arthur Stanleigh and be safe!" But "John Powell" was now on his way to Santa narbara, where he had arranged to take over a palatial steam yacht. Before leaving he had given orders that the men were to move to the south side of the mine and go on with the work at the new diggings.

Alone in Santa Barbara Vivian Marston was scheming how best she could bring the young mil- lionaire within the meshes of her net. Twice she had met him, once at Mrs. Randolph's ball and again at the Fairfax Tournament. These brief con- tacts were nothing. She felt that to impress Arthur she must throw herself in his way at a time when there would be no distraction to militate against the dramatic intensity of the meeting as she planned it. A daring scheme was formulating in her mind, and that Morning when Arthur left the hotel to go aboard the yacht, waiting in the bay with steam up. Vivian acted quickly. Driving to the wharf she hired a sailing-boat, and steered directly for the yacht. " John Powell " was at the wheel receiving his first instructions from the sail- ing master when to his horror he saw a sailing boat in charge of a woman laying a course directly across the sharp bow of the great yacht.

Scarcely had Esther and Quabba started for the mine than they were seen by Blair and Luke, who were quickly in pursuit. The keen eyes of the hunchback noted the danger, and, seizing Esther by the hand, he turned sharply down the rocky hillside, where the sheds at the mine mouth could be seen at the bottom of the wild gorge far below. Over rock and shrub, down the dizzy hillside, Quabba and Esther fled. But the more sturdy and agile Blair and Luke gained on them. Suddenly Quabba held himself and Esther back with an effort. They had reached the upper an- chorage of the cable carrier across the gorge. Here the empty ore cradle hung 'upon its pulleys, just as the ore strippers at the outcrop left it when sum- moned with other workmen to proceed through the tunnel in the mountains to the new workings on the other side.'

" Quick!" gasped • Quabba, clambering nimbly into the ore carrier, and helping Esther up beside him. Just as the hand of Luke Lovell clutched at the carrier,Quabba lifted the catch, and the aerial tram started across the cable and darted with in- creased momentum over the deep wild gorge and the rocky, Aurbtilent stream roaring beneath. Over the sickening height, borne by the thin line of the cable, went the frightened girl and the devoted Quabba, while the desperate Blair and his gipsy accomplice cursed each other and the flying fugi- tives on their swift and perilous flight. At the lower anchorage on the other side of the canyon the 'aerial tram stopped with a sudden shock that almost precipitatealts occupants to the ground. Recovering. Quabba, and Esther climbed out and hastened around from the tipple tracks and back to the other side of the gorge to the mine mouth. Luke and Blair Meanwhile had plunged down from the upper anchorage of the aerial tramway to the river an forded their way across as-best they could, and reached the other side only to see their qtarry doubling back to the tunnel mouth. But the peril of the fugitives was not yet over. The stronger pursuers were again gaining on them, and the out- look for the two was dark indeed.

DESPERATE CHANCES.

Blair Stanleigh had long since resolved to spare neither himself nor others in the search for The Diamond from the Sky, and now,he knew of the existence of the document which would reveal the secret so long treasured by enemies of his family. That the replacing of the broken linch-pin would send Esther and the English lawyer to an awful death did not trouble him. The crashing of the coach as it pounded down the hillside .was almost Music to his ears. If ever two men were guilty of Premeditated murder Blair and Luke were, and, eagerly anticipating a scene of death and destruc- tion, they scrambled down the steep hillside to the bottom, where lay the splintered Coach and the bodies of Esther and Marmaduke. But once again a vile plot had miscarried. While waiting for the repair to the wheel Esther caught sight of her two enemies, and although dazed from the shock of the fall of the coach was sufficiently conscious to hide the precious document and feign unconsciousness when the exultant villains came to the smashed coach. She heard Luke Lovell roar angrily: "Hang you, Blair Stanleigh! You knew Esther was in the coach. It wasn't the English lawyer You hired me to kill, then, though he lies dead enough to suit anybody. You tricked me, you gentleman blackguard. I wouldn't have a hair of her head harmed! I'll have your life for this." "I swear I-didn't see the girl; I was in the shop, you know, when the coach was stopped," lied Blair, glibly. "It was the English lawyer I was after. He is going to find Arthur Stanleigh. If Arthur Stanleigh goes hack to Virginia I shall swing for the murder of Dr. Lee." "You lie, and you know you lie," raged Luke. " The girl is dead. She would never look at me, but I have always loved her. I might have been a better man if she had cared for me." "You forget that I am fond of Esther, too," Murmured Blair. "But if she is dead, or if she is unconscious and lives, she will have no thought of you and me," added Main "It is she who has the Proof we seek to make our fortunes, the proof Arthur Stanleigh will give all the millions he has made as John Powell to keep suppressed. Esther has the Stanleigh document." and as he said this Blair stooped_ over 'the seemingly unconscious girl. "Don't you touch herl Don't you lay a hand on her!" cried Luke. " I'll carry her. She isn't dead, thank Heaven."

Esther stirred and sat up. gazing resolutely at them both. Luke picked her up as though she were a child. "You bring her baggage," he said roughly to Blair, and he nodded his head, indicating the

WARRINGTON AT LEIGH.

Heavy Reverse After A Promising Opening.

After resting all last season, Warrington visited Leigh on Saturday to play their opening match. Neither team was at full strength, Jolley being absent from the Warrington side, and several for- wards from the Leigh team. There were a few fresh faces in the Warrington team, whereas Leigh had practically all last season's players, in addition to their old half-back, Herbert Ganley, who has been playing with Huddersfield. He undoubtedly greatly strengthened the back division, which was mainly responsible for Warrington's downfall. The home team's passing was brilliant, and although the War- rington men played hard, they were easily out- pointed. Warrington Start Well, But Fall Away. The game started ten minutes late before about 1,600 spectators. Following a free kick against Leigh, Carter secured and started a passing move- ment, Ganley kicking into touch near• the War- rington line. Flom the scrimmage Ganley secured and made a fine bid for the Hike, being stopped, a few yards short. Warrington Managed to clear their lines, but by means of fine passing Leigh were soon back again, and O'Neil might have scored had not a poor pass been given to him. Still keeping up the attack Whalley made a good run, but Higha n brought him down. Following a fine pass- ing movement, O'Neil received, but was pushed into touch near the line, Tvinter clearing With a huge kick. Leigh were now playing hard to open their score. but the Warrington tackling was sound. Warrington had a turn on the attack, their forwards rushing in a body to the home line, where Clark- son picked up finely and cleared. A minnte later Whalley' had to clear- the home line from another big rush br.the visiting men. Ganley broke away in fine style, and after a good run kicked over the full-back's head, the ball rolling into touch. From the scrimmage the hbme backs started a fine pass- ing movement, which ended in Keegan scoring behind the posts, Whalley kicking the goal. War- rington attacked, and by means of short kicking and following up reached the Leigh quarters. Tranter made a high kick over the Leigh line, but Clark- son made the ball dead. Following th?e kick-out. Dainteth made a mark, but Taberner's kick at goal was a, failure. Leigh were hemmed in their quar- ters for some time, but eventually Carter and Keegan broke away, and Grundy joining in the movement, that player seored a fine- try in •the corner, but Whalley's goal kick was a failure. Leigh were soon again attacking, and from another passing movement O'Neil ran in, hut Whalley failed at goal.

Leigh. one goal three tries (11 points); Warrington, nil. Leigh Pile on the Agony.

The game was re-started after a long interval, and Leigh immediately visited the Warrington quarters. Here another passing movement was initiated by Ganley, and O'Neil was stopped on the line. By means of good forward work Warrington cleared their lines for a time, but Grundy. after a fine pass- ing movement, in which O'Neil, Carter, Ganley arid Keegan took part, ran over unopposed. Whalley converted, arid A Warrington were sixteen points to the bad. Leigh were now playing an excellent game, their running and passing being superb, and before the whistle sounded O'Neil, Ganley and Keegan got further tries, Whalley converting two of them. Tranter scored Warrington's only try from a passing bout. Higham failing to convert. Result: Leigt, four goals seven tries (29 points); Warrington, one try (three points). Players and Their

Ganley played well for Leigh, and with him at half-back Mooney combined splendidly. Clarkson also at full-back played a good game, kicking and tackling well. On the Warrington side, Wheeler—who volun- teered at the last moment to take up the full-back position, made vacant owing to Jolley missing his connection from Runcorn—was rather weak. Tranter and Baker did well, and Young and Dain- teth at times combined well together. The teams were as follows:

Warrington: Wheeler, back; iTaberner. Higham. Tranter and Baker. threequarteT-backs; Young and Dainteth, half-backs; Farmer, Cunliffe, , Green, Mathers, Clegg and Dagnall. forwards. Leigh: Clarkson, , back; O'Neil, Carter, Keegan and Grandy, threequarter-backs; Mooney and Gan- ley. half-backs; Boardman, Marsh; Shaw, -Holt, Gotheridge and Whalley, forwards. Referee: Mr. C. Platt (Swinton).

BROUGHTON AT NVILDERSPOOL TO-DAY.

To-day Warrington are at home to Broughton Rangers, and, given fine weather, a good game should be witnessed. Broughton were beaten at home by Barrow last Saturday by twelve points to five. WARRINGTON WORKING MEN'S ASSOCIATION.

N.A. CENTRE.

The inclemency of the weather upset all calcula- tions last week-end. Members were out for speci- mens at Chirk, Llangollen, Waverton, Hartford, Norton; and the Gowy, and the only fish to be 'placed on record were a perch of llfoz. from Hart- ford, a couple of dace from the Gowy of 9oz. and 81oz., and roach from Chirk ranging up to 10oz. The matches this week will overshadow all other fishing. The headquarters hold their annual match at Newbridge, and we are almost certain it will be a success. This contes is looked forward to as one of the great events of the season, and members are very keen to be amongst the prizes, which this year amount to £lO. A 'bus will leave •the Gladstone Arms at 7.30 a.m., September 10th. No member to Ifish Newbridge length on morning of the match. A.S.

LIQUID FIRE.

"Liquid Fire as a Weapon" forms the theme of an exceedingly interesting article in the current issue of the bright war chronicle, "The. War Budget." The diabolical contriv- ance is thus .described . "In the earliest models the combustible liquid was propelled by 'a gas-condenser out of a portable or fixed reservoir, and was lighted by some, automatic device as it escaped from the nozzle of the 'projecting instrument. With the instrument described, the flaming jet can make its effects felt at a distance of forty or forty- five yards, btit is not capable of exceeding that range effectively, because ..of the consumption of the liquid in transit. Later, 'a double- barrelled liquid-gun was devised, .having the upper barrel much smaller than the lower, and pivoted so as to turn independently. 'Tlje fluid is shot from he two barrels simultaneously, but only thht from the upper one ignites auto- matically. This small, burning stream is so directed that it unites with the larger, non- burning one at any desired point, and then, of course, ignites the large jet. The small stream is then shut off, the large one continu- ing to flow. The flames do not spread backward along tke.jet toward the nozzle. but are carried forward to the target, and, striking the ground, form a veritable sheet of fire, which continues to ignite the fluid as fast and as- long as it falls."
