ASSOCIATION. (Lancashire Combination.)

WARRINGTON TOWN.

File Goals Behind In The First Match At Tranmere.

Warrington Town opened the season with a visit to Tranmere. The team the Town fielded Lad but three new names—Billy Siddall, Kendrick and, Eccleston—all local lads. Siddall has been secured from the Eccles Borough Club, with whom he has played for sevral seasons, and has a good repu- tation. Kendrick is cn old Blue Coat School boy and enlisted in n.M. Forces shortly after the out- break of war. Efforts are being made to secure his release frcm the Army each Saturday afternoon tct enable him to assist thp Town team. Eccleston has played for junior teams in Warrington. The Rovers won the toss and kicked off before 3,000. spectators. Immediately the Town took up the attack, `Eceleston and Kendrick making great strides for Gallagher, when ,the home defence robbed them of what appeared to be a certain goal and sent the ball up the field. Soon afterwards the Town resumed the siege, Kendrick; Ditchfield and Aspinall working well together, and many shots were aimed, but Gallagher rose to the occa- sion. Two corners given to the Town were taken by Eccleston) but each sent behind. The Rovers now got to work and harassed the Town defence, the forwards straining every nerve to improve 'matters. but Leah and Cookson were up to the mark and playing soundly.,

Just before the interval the ball was passed out to Millward, who shot with deadly aim. A goal seemed absolutely certain, when the ball t'ot into contact with the features of a Tranrnere defender. This 'wris exceediinly unlucky for the Town. The Rovers, froth the clearance, made a bee-line for kite, when an appeal was ,made .by the Town defence ;for off-side, but was unheeded by the referee. Cookson, seeing the position, rushed in rather vigorously and upset Williamson When he was iust about to " pop " at goal. The action met with the disapproval of the referee, and he awarded the Rovers a penalty, from which Bull scored. Half-time: Tranmere Rovers, 1 goal; Warrington Town, nil.

Aspinall kicked off amid great excitement amongst the crowd, many of whom thought the Town would prove the victors by a substantial mdrgin, but it was soon in evidence who were the fitter team. However, Warrington again attacked, but their shooting was extremely weak. Several exchanges took place and the Rovers were now on their mettle and repeatedly attacked, Moreton and Cunningham standing out very prominently, and Ditchfield was hard pressed and unable to cope with their determined ,efforts. Many times Kite was called upon to handle, and twice or three times only saved at the expense of corners. It was now about twenty minutes off time and it was quite clear the. Town were worn out and could not stay the,course, it being now quite plain to notice the superiority of the Rovers. Their sprightliness and sustained aggressiveness at this stage clearly proved what preparation they had undergone. The Town goal was never out of difficulty, and, as far as the Town was concerned, attacking tactics were finished. All they could do was to save a big score against them. The " tit-bit "of the after- noon was the fourth goal. The Town had managed to raise a gallop and made away to the home goal- mouth, but the ball was returned. Cunningham then gained possession near the half-way line and raced right ,hrotr•li the Town 'team and scored a magnificent goal. \

Final :—Tranroere Rovers, 5 goals; Warrington Town, nil.

REASON FOR DEFEAT: LACK OF TRAINING. Though the Town were defeated by five goals it was only in the last twenty minutes of the game that four of them were registered, but Tranmere were well worthy of the victory. They are a great side this year. That they were the better team was largely due to the fact that during the last four weeks they have put in some useful training and their' manner clearly showed what they had under- gone. They were easily more sprightly than the Town, who, by comparison, , were "by, no means fitted for the fray. The game was full of excite- ment and it kept over 3,000 spectators interested. Warrington, throughout the 'first half, showed up well, and monopolised the play. Many thought that the Town ought 'to have had three goals in the first half; in fact, two were possibly in the net, but did not catch 'the referee's eye. Kite was not in his usual form and was some- times erratic. Leah and Cookson were very sound indeed and never faltered in their play. The half- backs require a good deal of lubricating and could do with a month's training. The forwards played very well. It was Eccleston's trial; one's impres- sions of him are very slight. He may turn out good some day. Kendrick also made his debut and very promising it was, too; he ought to make a tip-topper. As to Aspinall, Siddall and Mill- ward, one need not say anything of their play, except that it was to this trio that the Rovers' spectators began to look for trouble—but, alas, 'twas in vain!

ASSOCIATION NOTES.

The football season of 1916-17 opened very quietly in the Warrington district. The Juniors were con- tent in playing trial games in order to help their committees to select their strongest elevens for their opening League games, which commence to- day. Plenty of enthusiasm was shown by players wishing to secure their places.

Members and supporters of the Excelsior and the Stockton Heath Clubs will be delighted that Sam Lloyd, who had previously been one of their lead- ing players, has been given a trial with the Everton Club, and showed such splendid form that he was unhesitatingly selected for the outside right posi- tion in the "Blues' opening League fixture last week at Gigg-lane against Bury. Lloyd gave a very satisfactory display, and if he maintains his present form he should be able to keep his position in the team.

Another local junior, in the person of Harold Eccleston, made his debut in the Combination circles with the Town Club last week at Prenton Park against Tranmere Rovers. His display was very promising, and should ensure him an extended trial. Harold has the necessaiy speed and height in his favour, and when he has a little more experi- ence and gains confidence with -himself he should prove a very useful player for the Town Club. He formerly played for Bank Quay Recs. and the Longford United Club.

Tommy Kendrick is another ' local youth who assisted the Town Club last week and formerly played With the Sacred Heart and ,the Elmwood Congregationalists, but he greatly developed his talents after joining the King's Own, for which team he played with success last season. He gave a very creditable display against the Town Club last season in ihe charity match, when the King's Own were the visitors to the Hood-lane ground.

Latchford Cadets.

The Cadets last season experienced many difficul- ties as to ground accommodation and several other unforeseen circumstances, and were not able to com- plete their fixtures, but now their membership has been greatly increased and they are doing some very useful work in various ways for the local authorities in aiding the sick and wounded soldiers; and also preparing themselves for duty when called upon. They have decided to re-organise their Football Club to provide some recreation for their members. They nave joined the Warrington and District Junior League again, and elected the following as office- bearers for the season: President, Mr. J. B. Starkey; treasurer, Mr. E. Lee; secretary, Mr. W. \I ‘ft Ni\ ORKERS' RECREATION.

Lea wky For Warrington And District.

On Monday night a meeting was held at the Mersey Hotel for the purpose of forming a Foot- ball League to provide some recreation for those who are engaged on munition work in the town and district. There was a fair attendance of dele- gates, and it was decided to call the new competi- tion the Warrinzton and District Alliance.

Owing to the late start with this competition it was decided to play friendly games during the first month, and another meeting will be held at the Mersey Hotel on the 18th to arrange League fixtures to commence in October., ,The entries include clubs in the town, Earlestown and Runcorn, and further applications are invited. The honorary secretary (Pro— tem.) is Mr. J. Smith, 7, Chapel-lane, Stockton Heath, Warrington.

FARM AND GARDEN.

REGENERATED WHEAT,

New Breeds Of Seed That Give Impetus

To Cultivation.

'As soon as the harvest is finished the minds of most agriculturists will be turning towar ' wheat cropping for next year, and, as usu; at this season, Messrs. Gartons Limited, tl well-known seed experts, of Warrington, ha' issued their new catalogue of new and regene) ated breeds of wheats and other autumn sowir seeds. It is an admirable publication in evei way, and we can commend it to the attenti, of all progressive agriculturists. It is admitted on all hands that the adv2n of the new regenerated breeds which have bee) raised by this enterprising firm have brough great favour to wheat culture in this country and there is no doubt that the heavy yieldin,, strong strawed varieties which are so mud superior to the old-fashioned sorts grown year: ago, will help materially to foster the wish, grow more wheat at home. ,Messrs. Gartons give timely advice to farmers to use sulphate of ammonia as a fertiliser at the time of ,sow- ing, and have furthermore impressed upon farmers the desirability of sowing more winter grain (wheats, oats, and baileys). Advantages of Winter Sowing.

It is rightly pointed out in the publication that the Advantages of winter sowing over spring sowing are manifold. In the first place, winter sowing .helps to relieve the pressure of spring work, which is in many cases more than most farmers with depleted staffs can deal. with conveniently at the present time. The latitude of sowing winter grain is much greater than in the case of spring sowing, as sowing can take place practically from I.y. time, when weather permits, from September to February, as many. months in fact as weeks in spring. Just as important is the fact that winter-sown crops of oats and barleys are always ready for harvest- ing before spring crops, and can .Sometimes- even be stacked before .spring crops are cut. Need- less to say, the catalogue gives full particulars of all the firm's . new and regenerated breeds of seeds for autumn sowing, - pre-eminence being given to the firm's latest wheats, including "Benefactor" and "Victor," the • heaviest yielding and best paying wheats at present in cultivation. -

No reader, should fail to read- a copy of this catalogue, -which can be obtained post free on application to the firm's headquarters at War- rington.

WOMEN IN AGRICULTURE.

Grants For Farmers Who Will Take Pupils.

The extension of. the work of the Cheshire County Committee for promoting the employ- ment of women in agriculture during the war was -considered at a meeting held in •Crewe this week, when Lord Newton was in the chair. This committee has been working in the county for considerably over a year and it is felt that there is room for development. There is need for closer co-operation between the General Committee and the numerous voluntary workers who are assisting in the villages. The Hon. Secretary . (the I-ion. John E. 'Cross) at the meeting put forward a scheme for the establishment of sub-committees for the Hundreds of Cheshire. This proposal was ap- proved by the committee, and a resolution was Passed at tile meeting advocating the formation of sub-committees. - Steps will therefore be taken to put the new scheme into force as soon as possible. -

The Cheshire Education Authority, in con- junction with the Board of Agriculture and this committee, have arranged three new train- ing schemes on the following lines:— (a) Resident Scheme, whereby farmers .may receive a grant of 12s. per week for two weeks and 10s. per week for four subsequent weeks in return - for providing board, lodging and in- struction in general farm work. (b) Non-Resident Scheme; whereby farmers may receive a grant of 9s. per week for two weeks if he is willing to provide board, lodging and instruction for four weeks.

(c) Milking Scheme.—ln this connection a grant of 7s. 6d. per .head will he made, to ;far- mers willing to provide 30 milking lessons per pupil. Farmers who are willing to assist the com- mittee by; providing the necessary facilities for training should communicate with the lion. John E. Cross, West Hall, High Legh, IC/14s- ford.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0146.txt | included=true | included =====
Satisfactory Progress.

The training at the Henhull Hall Farm progressing most satisfactorily, and the Farm Manager, under whose supervision the women receive their instruction, iS very Well . satisfied with the ,progress made. Some of these' workers are still available and further particulars may also be obtained from the Hon. Secretary. Mr. Cross reported that he-had received most encouraging letters from some of the students trained at Henhull who were proving to be of real use to farmers in the present, emergency.

STORING FRUIT.

Underground stores are best for maintaining fruit in sound condition over the longest period, and these, provided % with a span roof and a double range of shelves, are easily made at the minimum of expense. Cellars, if not damp, also serve as fruit stores—in fact; so long as darkness, and an 'even, cool temperature can be provided, it matters litho- whether the structure is one specially built or merely adapted. Draughts are inimical to the proper keeping of fruit, so that whatever means are designed for securing ventilation, they must be such as will serve to renew the atmosphere without a sudden inrush of cold air. If pack- ing material is used to line the shelves, it must be of a nonjheating character—clean, hi rd- stemmed straw is better than anything, and it should be laid lo4ly but evenly, and .the fruit spread in a single layer upon it. The less juicy varieties of apples can be •spread in g double layer to economise space, and the latest kinds will take no harm if heaped one foot deep provided that air can pass between them. This is secured by liaving the shelves made in- wooden slats. Fruit in store must be examined at frequent intervals, as. no matter how care- fully it has been looked over in the first in- stance, •a few damaged specimens are sure irc be included. The decay-spores on over-ripe fruit are exceedingly active, and are readily carried on the faintest breath of air to spread Ithe infection elsewhere.—" Farm and Home."

THE EXAMINER, SATURDAY, SEPTEMBER 9, 1916.

Medical.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0166.txt | included=true | included =====
IN THE COURTS.

FINED FOR KEEPING UNLICENSED PIGEONS.

Inspector Moffatt told the magistrates at the County Petty Sessions on Wednesday that the military authorities had instructed him to take proceedings in a charge against a young fellow named George Risley, of Warrington-road, Penketh, for keeping homing pigeons without a licence and removing them from WaiTington to Penketh without a permit.

The defendant pleaded guilty. Mr. F. A. Frost (chairman) said that under the Defence of the Realm Regulations defend- ant could be fined £lOO and given six months' imprisonment, but he would only be fined 20s. on this occasion. These regulations were very seriously made and the magistrates must up- hold them.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0167.txt | included=true | included =====
A MARE'S SORES.

Though Mr. Arthur Browne, solicitor, arguefl on behalf of Messrs. W. and J. Richardson, coal merchants, that the sores had healed but broken out again while the animal was walking, George Blease, of 37, Wakefield-street, was fined 55., and 'Jesse Roberts, foreman horsekeeper, of 57, Price-street, was fined 10s., for ill-treating a mare by working it in an ,unfit condition. Inspector Bonham, of the R.S.P.C.A.. proved the charge, stating that a sore as large as a half-crown was being rubbed by the collar, and another like a florin was under the saddle. Mr. Browne poinbed out that the company took great care of their horses, and that was the first time they had been concenr,d in a Case of that character. They wanted to maintain the good reputation they had got.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0168.txt | included=true | included =====
MAKING IT TOO HOT FOR SLACKERS.

"We are making Warrington too hot for these men," remarked Captain McPhail, at the Borough Police Court on Tuesday, with refer- ence to three men of no settled abode, charged with failing to report for service before Auguit 31st. He added that such nien usiially went from town to .town as each place became too hot for them. Finding no one would employ them, they had been compelled to come to the Police Station, but ought to have done so before. In two of the cases fines of 10s. were imposed, but' all three prisoners' were handed over to the military authorities.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0169.txt | included=true | included =====
ZEPP FINES.

For failing to subdue lights, the following were fined at the Borough Police Court on Tuesday :—Joseph Caldwell, 157, Winwick-road, 10s.; William Green, 136, Ellesmere-street, 55.; and Louis Lewis, 104, Winwick-street, 20s.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0170.txt | included=true | included =====
COST OF LIVING AND OLD-AGE

PENSIONS.

At Tuesday's meeting of Runcorn Board of Guardians a letter from the West Ham Guard- ians asked for support of this resolution: "That as official records agree that the Turchasinc, power of a sovereign in the case of food stuffs has been reduced by at least 50 per cent., the Government be called upon to increase the old- age pension to a maximum of at least 7s. 6d.; that the receipt of Poor Law relief shall not disqualify old-age pensioners from continuing to receive a pension; that in the case of old-age pensioners receiving assistance in Poor Law in- stitutions the pensions to be paid to the Guard- ians concerned towards the cost of the mainten- ance of the pensioners.

Mr. Wright moved that the resolution be sup- ported. Mr. Linaker, seconding, said the Government were taking this matter into consideration. They could, however, signify their approval of the object of the resolution. The resolution was approved.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0171.txt | included=true | included =====
WORKHOUSE INFIRMARY.

Transformed Into Military Hospital

At Dutton.

The 'newly-appointed Military Hospital Com- mittee, of which Mr. C. E. Linaker is chairman, has already held two meetings, and on Tuesday ,reported progress to the Runcorn Board of Guardians. Their minutes stated that the Local Government Board had written approving the Proposal to hand over the Infirmary to the Military Authorities for the accommodation of wounded soldiers, and expressing their, appre- ciation of the patriotic action of the Guardians. A letter had also been received from the mili- tary Authorities undertaking to be responsible for the cost of equipping the Hospital with beds, bedding, &c. and for the wages of the staff, but intimated that they would not be respon- sible for any payments to the Matron, Medical Officer, Master, or additional clerical staff. It was decided by the committee that Mr. Leonard Ratcliffe be appointed, officer in charge of the Military Hospital; Mrs. E. J. Parsons, matron; and Dr. T. J. Selby, medical officer. The committee further appointed a' charge nurse, a night nurse and assistant nurses, an assistant cook, wardsmaids and scrubbers, and completed arrangements for the supply of meat, groceries, and other necessaries. Fittings for the Hospital were also provided. Mr. Woodward asked how many beds it was proposed to have in the Hospital, and the Clerk said seventy or seventy-two. Mr. Woodward said in that case he thought that another medical man should be associated with Dr. Selby, and he suggested that Dr. Bowers be asked to assist. •I • Mr. Linaker said that was the intention of the committee, and with this addition the i.minutes were approved.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0172.txt | included=true | included =====
A Timely Appeal.

It is expected that Inmates for the new Host pital will begin to arrive in about a week's time. Although the :matter was not mentioned at the Board meeting, it should be known that the Military Authorities do riot provide cigar- ettes, tobacco, or other little luxuries. Mrs. E. J. Parsons, the matron, therefore makes An appeal to the generously Inclined for gifts of this nature.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0173.txt | included=true | included =====
To-morrow.

No one ever sinks under the burden of to-day; it is always the to-morrow and yesterdays that overwhelm us. If it pere not for the sorrows of the past and our fears and anxieties for the future, we could very well bear to-day's cares and do its work. Every day holds its strength and its compensations as well as its troubles, but it has no provision for that which does not belong to it. All the journey, through the heaven-sent manna must be gathered morning by morning.—" Great Thoughts."

This week several sdmirers from the audience have handed her boxes of chocolates, which have led her to remark that last year during six weeks she spent in Dublin, admirers troubled the conductor to pass along to her no fewer than 105 boxes. The following week at Cork, where a large number of troops were stationed, the front rows were crowded with officers, she received no fewer than 130 of 'these tasty souvenirs. Not being a mere sweetmeat digester, and, moreover, having a kind dis- position (at one time her parents resided in Warrington). Miss Allen sent many pounds along to the hospitals..

. . A masterpiece of stage production is to be seen next week, when "Sealed Orders.." will be the order. It has many scenes and is a drama of topical moment. Dramatic incidents succeed one anothir incessantly. Everything is on a grand scale, and the show will be the talk GC the town next week. Mi. IT.. L. Warburton,-tho manager, is to be highly congratulated on his enterprise, for we have had a splendid series of productions since he took charge.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0174.txt | included=true | included =====
STAGE AND KINEMA.

Royal Court Theatre. As I foreshadowed last week, " Knick- Knacks " has turned out a highly popular revue. With a first-class cast, well-contrived episodes, and a real beauty chorus (not alleged) it has gairml very pleasant effects all weop.-

Mr. George Hestor is a comedian of parts. He has had long experiences on the stage, hav- ing played in all sorts of theatrical works. On one occasion, through other members of a com- pany falling ill, he played no fewer than seven parts in the " Silver King" in one night—" a bit of a record I should think," he remarked when telling me of the incident. Until three weeks ago, when he took a fortnight off to rest at his bungalow on the Thames, Mr. Hestor had not had a holiday for seventeen months.

"This .revue, he says, "has always gone strong. In my opinion, revues will last as long as the war lasts. The reason is that they are light and airy, and take people out of them- selves. But after the war. when all the boys come back and the old family 'life is, resumed, the good old drama will come into its own again."

As the leading lady, Miss Beatride Allen has scored with eminent success. She has been with the company ten weeks, coming here from "This is the Life" to succeed Miss Ouida McDermott. Three years' experience in revue has sealed her fame as an ,artiste. At Christ- mas time she usually plays principal boy, last year being at the New Queen's Theatre, Man- chester.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0175.txt | included=true | included =====
Palace and Hippodrome.

Although poetry sometimes plays a very im- portant part in love affairs, a potted musical breach of promise case is a decided novelty. With its presentation by Mr. Charles Hutchins's company Of entertainers, "The Q's,", everyone seemed pleased, except the defendant, who was mulcted in a thousand pounds damages. Quito a humber of lines of popular songs were appropriately introduced into the burlesque. Up-to-date marionettes are cleverly worked. Human heads surmounting diminutive bodies make a most diverting show. As vocalists The Q's attain marked excellence.

The ,Miles-Stavordale Quintet perform on sweet-timed instruments of the mandoline-banjo type, which reproduce the human voice with remarka.)le accuracy and effect. One of the number is an accomplished harpist.

Rene Ralph has made a special hit with her cheery ballad which she sings with much spirit, "Pack up your troubles in your old kit bag and smile, smile, smile." The house chorused vigorously. ,

Jack Walsh, who-styles himself a little bunch 'of comedy, deserves the appellation. , Nothing could catch on, better than his funny contribu- tidn, "What a pal!" Clifford and Grey, clever hoop jugglers; Agda and Company, novel acrobats; and Amos Howard, the dancing darkie, all met with approval. The Hippodrome pic- ture this week is exceptionally interesting,' showing the ghastly battle ground of Neuve Chappelle—a desolate, dreary Prb4pect with those terrible waterlogged trenches where so many of our :gallant lads fought and fell. ,

It was pleasing to hear the hippodrome orchestra, under Mr. T. H. Hulley, applauded so vigorously for their .selections. It was well deserved. Quite 'a feature of the interval was Mr. J. Crabtree's Piccolo solo, " L'Oiseau du Bois." It was rendered with much vivacity and skill.

Another splendid programme is to be pro- duced next week, several well-known artistes being included in the programme. We are to have some -singing by Rosie and Mons Dubin, the last named being a famous Russian tenor. Joe and Betty Binns are to present us with comedy as well as music. Alcide Capitaine is to give a study of grace and physical culture, while the fun will be supplied by George Hyam. Others included in the programme are "Three Bros. Huxter," Lillie Langtry, a smart comedienne; Doris, a young violinist; and Monty McVean, a Scotch character vocal comedian.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0176.txt | included=true | included =====
Klnemas.

There is now showing at the Star Kinema Heuse, Church-street, a fine comedy, "The Submarine Pirate," featiqing. Syd Chaplin. It it the first Triangle film to be shown in War- rington, and is attracting crowded houses. For the first half of next week we are to have that much talked of film, "The Battle of the Somme." Seats are now being booked and patrons should 'consult our advertisement columns for extra times .of showing. For the latter half a thrilling 'drama, "The Golden Chance," will be screened.

At the Grand Electric Theatre,. Florence Turner is now appealing in "Doorsteps," along with a fine film entitled, "A Child of the Wilderness," depicting jungle life. During the first half of net week Blanche, Sweet will be the central figure in a fine picture, ". The Secret Orchard," and the -fifteenth episode of the "Diamond From the Sky" will also be shown. For the latter half "Man and His Soul" is the title of the picture which will top a fine programme.

"No Greatier Love and the sixth adventure of "Terence O'Rourke" are the " star " pi.. tures now being shown at the Pavilion, Sankey Green. For the first half of next week a lovely exclusive photo play is annolinced, entitled "The Miracle of Life," along with the fifteenth

About 150 Cadets paraded, the following companies being represented,i—St. Clement's, St. Elphin's, St. Paul's, Padgate, and Earles- town; with the officers: Major T. A. Brookes, Major Ford, Captain Bunch, Captain Simpson, Lieutenant Morris, Lieutenant Toft, Sergeant- Major Goodwin, and Instructor Essenhigh. The Band of the St. Paul's Company was in attend- ance.

Colonel Buckton was received with a general salute and after prayers he inspected the Bat- talion in line. Then followed company drill, arms inspection, Swedish drill, and signalling and ambulance work, the two latter by sections from St. Paul's Company. ‘Colonel BUckton, addrefssing the Cadets, said although he no longer lived in Warrington, he always • took the kindest interest in the work of the Battalion to which he was nominally attached. He wished to say how pleased he• Was to see the lads looking so smart and soldierly in their khaki uniforms- The drills were, ex- ceptionally well done. He had inspected lads and' men on many occasions and had never seen a steadier parade. Having impressed upon them the significance of their uniform—the same as that worn by the men at the Front— he urged them to stick to their work.

Liverpool Olympia.

" Razzle Dazzle," the super revue, is coming to Liverpool on Monday, opening at the Olympia. Rightly described as the most stupendous spectacular production ever staged at Drury Lane, " Razzle Dazzle" will astonish the provinces as it has London by its magnifi- cence. " Razzle Dazzle" is a revelation In revue, and a top-notch effort. Liverpool's " Razzle Dazzle" will be an exact replica of the original which is now playing at.the Empire Theatre, London. The chief comedian will be Herbert Darnley, whose fun making propensities are widely .known theatreland. It was Mr. Darnley who wrote all Dan Leno's greatest successes. Beauty will be represented by the ever-charming Edna Morgan, and the other lady principals will be Gladys Wise, Nellie Sheffield and Violet Cooney. There are also to be 150 of Britain's most beautiful chorus girls.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0181.txt | included=true | included =====
MRS. JOSEPH ADAMS.

Death 01 Wife Of Well-known Medical

Practitioner.

Much regret has been occasioned by the death, after a short illness, of Mrs. Adams, wife of Dr. Joseph Adams, 93, Bewsey-street. Early in last week she was apparently in her usual health, but illness came suddenly and she passed away on Saturday night. Of too retiring a nature to be prominent in the town, Mrs. Adams was; however, highly esteemed by a large circle of friends. She came to Warrington about 33 years ago, when the doctor started to practise here soon after their marriage. 'She was born in Edinburgh 58 years ago. As a vocalist and pianist, as well as a conversationalist, she was an interesting hostess, and her friendship was much valued. For many years she attended St. Paul's Church, but latterly worshipped at Holy Trinity Church. The interment took place in Lymm Church- yard on Tuesday, afternoon, the ceremony being conducted by the Rev. E. C.- E. 'Carleton, vicar of Holy Trinity Church. The principal mourners were: Dr. Adams (husband), Mr. Harvey Orr Adams (son), Miss Adams and Mrs. Jackson (St. Helens); daugh- ters; Mrs. Alec Harman (cousin), Mr. Spencer Adams (brother-in-law), Mr. Gilbert P. MILITARY TRIBUNALS.

SERVED IN BOER WAR.

Buiider To Serve In European

War Also.

Dr. Joseph (Deputy Mayor), Alderman Tinnion and Messrs. F. A. Frost, J. J. Whitley and W. Bradbury composed yesterday's Tri- bunal in the Town Hall. Several appeals on be. half of young single men were refused. Mr. T. S. gteel, solicitor, appeared for two brothers who carry on the business of build- ing -Contractors. One is 39 years of age and unmarried, and the other is 38, married, with one child. At the outbreak of war they had 42 men employed, and noiV they have only eight. ,The elder brother's duties are those of buyer, casting up hills of quantities, measuring and settling all contract work with the archi- tects. He has also-the general internal manage- ment of the business and acts as book-keeper, there being no clerical staff. The other brother is the outside manager, who has control of the men and general supervision. The foremen had all gone, and the brothers had to act in that capacity. Both had been through the South African War—one had been in the Volun- teers for 15 years and the other for six years. One of the applicants said the business had been built up during the past 18 nears, and they had bath served their country once. They were entitled to consideration with other firms who have two left to carry on this class of business. The married partner was granted conditional exemption, but the other appeal was dismissed. Expiring Badge.

A pattern-maker in a Warrington • firm, engaged on war work, and whose certificate and badge expire on the 12th September, asked for exemption. He is 37, married, and with two children and widowed mother to. support:—The Tribunal conditionally refused the appeal—that was to say he would belong to the Army, but would probably, remain at his present work.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0183.txt | included=true | included =====
Refused.

Mr. W. H. J. Jenkins, solicitor, appeared for a married man, aged-28 years, employed in the grocery and provision trade, who applied for exemption for domestic reasons.—The appeal was refused.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0184.txt | included=true | included =====
Well-known Footballer.

The licensee of a public-house who is also a well-known footballer, again appealed for exemption.—The application was refused. The proprietor of three pawnbroking estab- lishments appealed for exemption for the man in charge of the shop in Knutsford-road. He had formerly six asistants employed, but four had gone into the Army.—Conditional exemption was granted—man to join the V.T.C. or serve as Special Constable. Further Exemption.

A master clogger, aged 21, who had been exempted until August 31st, told the Tribunal that he had only one man working for him. He cduld not get apprentices because boys were being paid better in munition works.— Further exemption was given until Ist October. Messrs. J. B. Johnson and Co., a firm of plasterers, appealed on behalf. of a foreman plaster, aged 34, who is at present engaged on work al a convent. The representative of the firm said that officers were wanted in the Army, and they must have foremen in the in- dustrial army.—Exemption was granted to fltl of December.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0185.txt | included=true | included =====
THE CHESHIRE SIDE.

Variety Of Applications From Rural ,Places.

Applications from Stockton Heath and neigh- bourhood were heard at the Runcorn Rural Tribunal, held at the Workhouse, Dutton, on Thursday, Mr. C. E. Linaker presiding

===== 0002601_19160909_art0186.txt | included=true | included =====
Exemptions.

Exemptions were granted as under : Thelwall gardener, aged 39, employed by Mrs. Bleekly, exempted, but ordered to get work on munitions before October Ist.

An employe of an Acton Grange farmer, who said soldiers, who needed fetching from the station and paying, were no good to him. A cowman employed by Sir Gilbert Greenall, Bart., from whose estate it was said over 20 men had enlisted.

A Stockton Heath_ hay and straw merchant

A Thelwall farm hand

An Appleton farmer I.**ho got an exemption for his teamsman stated that he had several Stockton Heath women working for him.

Offered to Join.

A painter and motor driver from Moore, represented by Mr. Linaker, offered to 'join the Army on the Ist December.—This length of time was allowed him.

To Be Medically Examined.

A wholesale fish and fruit dealer from Stock- ton, Heath was ordered to be medically examined, and a similar decision was given in the case of a Preston Brook grocer.

===== 0002601_19160909_art0187.txt | included=true | included =====
Hatton Collector Of Silver Bullets.

Mr. H. J. Westbrook applied for conditional exemption on behalf of a collector of taxes in Hatton and adjoining parishes, and tithes in 23 parishes. The applicant was also assistant overseer. In support of the application, letters wee read from the Clerk to the Income Tax Commissioners (Mr. Hy. Greenall) and the Sur- veyor of Taxes (Mr. W. A. Brooke). Mr. Westbrook said his client was, he contended. already engaged in war work as he was collect- ing the "silver bullets" as munitions. The applicant was a Volunteer. He had been before the Medical Board at Chester and was certified for garrison duty abroad. The submission was that the applicant would be better employed in his present work than in foreign garrison work. He was prepared to work at a munition works for three days a week.—The Tribunal granted conditional egemption—applicant to mit in three days a week at some munition works. ,

===== 0002601_19160909_art0188.txt | included=true | included =====
RURAL DISTRICT.

Orford Contractor's Fourth Son

To Join Up.

The Warrington Rural Tribunal gat on Wed- nesday, Mr. Geo. Mather in the chair. op An Orford contractor again appealed on be- half of his son, aged 23, unmarried, The young man, he stated, is indispensable to' him, especially in connection with a contract at present being fulfilled—making new machine. pits and large filter bed; p&c., for Orford Tannery. Applicant has thtee sons already in the Army. One of these wmo —contied three times, and had gone out again to France. Appli. cant thought it was sufficient to send three sons to the Army.—The appeal was dismissed. A vegetable, fruit and flower'grower, who had been granted exemption until the epd of August, appealed for an extension of time in order to give him an opportunity of clearing the groAnd. He is a Married man, 39 years of age.—The time was extended until 18th October, and when applicant was informed of the decision he remarked that there were plenty of young unmarried men who should be called up instead of men like him.

The application of a Penketh chemical worker, who appealed on domestic groun Is, was dismissed. He had been before the Medical Board, who told him to come up again on August 19th for re-examination, but he had failed to do so.

A coal dealer, aged , 40, married, five chil- dren, who has been in business sixteen years, was granted conditional exemption. A similar ruling was made in the case of a shopman in a Penketh butcher's establishment. tie is 39 years of age, and a married man with three children.

CHESHIRE STUDENTS FOR MONTREAL.

An Inter-University Scheme.

The Cheshire County Council 'Education Committee have macle arrangements with the authorities of McGill University, Montreal, 'which will permit of young engineering students winning technological scholarships in Cheshire going to Montreal to have part of their three years' training at the McGill Uni- versity. It is understood that the arrangement is reciprocal, and that McGill students will he accepted at English universities for a portion of their training.

COUNTY BOROUGH, OF WARRINGTON. WAR CHARITIES ACT, 1916. 1. The Act provides in Section 1 (1) that it shall not be lawful to make a public appeal for money or articles in kind for any War Charity as defined by the Act, or to attempt to raise money by a bazaar, sale, entertainment or exhi- bition, or by ally similar means for any such Charity unless -(1) the Charity is registered under the Act, and (2) the Charity has approved of such appeal or attempt to raise money. Any contravention of this provision is an offence against the Act and is .punishable on summary conviction by a• fine of not more than ..tlOO or imprisonment with or without hard labour for not more than three months. 2.. Applications for registration or exemption must be made to the Town Council, and must be made in the forms prescribed by the Charity Commissioners, copies of which can be obtained from the Town Clerk. Upon registration or exemption of a Charity a certificate of registra- tion or exemption will be given to the Charity. 3. The Regulations made by the Charity Com- missioners and approved by the Secretary of State provide that no Charity may be exempted from the provisions of Section 1 of the Act unless the scope of its operations as regards the amount of subscriptions expected to be obtained, the duration of the Charity or the area of collection or benefit is so limited as in the opinion of the Town Council to make it unnecessary in the public interests that the Charity should be Registered under the Act. J. LYON WHITTLE, Town Clerk. Town Clerk's Office, 9th September, 1916. '

WARRINGTON UNION. TENDERS. Persons willing to contract for the supply of any of the undermentioned articles, to be delivered free of expense at the Workhouse, Warrington, or Cottage Homes, Padgate, at such times, and in such quantities, as the Guardians or their officers may require, are requested to send in Tenders to me on or before 10 a.m., Wednesday, the 20th September, 1916, and the same will be considered by the Guardians at a meeting to be held on the same date. Tenders are required : From Ist October, 1916, to 31st September, 1917—Sweeping Chimneys. From Ist October, 1916, to 31st March, 1917:— Coal and Slack Tea *Crockeryware Butchers' Meat Boots, Shoes & Slippers Flour and Oatmeal Coffins Drapery Brooms and Brushes Wines and Spirits Leather. d; Shoemakers' Drugs. Necessaries *Surgical Dressings Tailors' Goods Soda Water Bacon, Butter, Cheese, Paints, &c. • Lard, Margarine, &c. *lronmongery Tobacco and Snuff Thirds Fish and Ice Beer and Stout Grocdries & Necessaries *Electrical Goods. Bovril or Oxo *Kitchen Utensils. *NO GOODS OF GERMAN OR AUSTRIAN ORIGIN WILL BE ACCEPTED. Separate tenders must be sent for the Work- house and Cottage Homes, and samples of the articles required can be seen at either institu- tion between the hours of 10 to 12 noon and 2 to 4 p.m. The fuel to be " supplied must be accord- ing to the specifications as set out On the tender form, and quotations must be made for delivery, either to the siding adjoining the Workhouse, or by cart, and by cart to the Cot- tage Homes. Forms of tender, and instructions to persons tendering (which must be strictly complied with), may be obtained from the undersigned. All accounts will be paid monthly. is now in hospital at Chelsea, making,satoisfac- tory progress. ,Second-Lieutenant Parsons, who is twenty- one years of age, obtained his commission OCtober,. and had been on active service nine weeks. PRIVATE W. CLEMENTS. Wounded.

CORPORAL J. FINNERTY. Fast Healing Arm Will Soon Be Well. Gassed in October, 1915, Corporal • John Finnerty, of the Royal Engineers, son of Mr. John Finnerty, 14, Portland-street, has now been wounded and is in hospital in France. The news that Corporal Finnerty had been wounded in the left arm was first conveyed on

- • ' ,SY4 • • 6:l°' tv;/„. , • (4t. . . ,• , •

a hospital card sent by a chaplain, while a letter was 'afterwards received from Corporal Finnerty himself. "I am going on fine," he wrote, "and I expect to be fit again in a few days, for my arm is healing up fine. As you will see by the address, I am in a hospital down at the base. I am being well looked after by the nurses. I am looking forward to the time when this lot will be all over and I am back home with you all." Corporal Finnerty, who is in his twenty- fourth year, enlisted in the Lancashire Fusiliers a, month 'after the outbreak of war, and after- wards transferred to the Royal Engineers. He has been on active service about fourteen months. He was formerly employed at the Latchford Tube Works, and as a boy attenkd St. Mary's C. School. LANCE-CORPORAL P. LAWLESS. LANCE-CORPORAL PETER, LAWLESS. Wounded. street, has been wounded for the second time, ptid is now in hospital at Sheffield. He was visited on Monday by , his mother, who found him progressing 4avourably from a wound in the left hand. He was previously wounded in the arm about twelve months ago. Lance-Corporal Lawless, who is in his twenty- fourth year, enlisted upon the outbreak of war and has been in France .about fifteen months. Ile .was formerly employed by Messrs. Rylands Bros., Ltd., and as a boy attended St. Marys R.C. School. His brother, Thomas Henry, of the A.S.C.; has been invalided home from France, while another one, Frank, who also served at the Front with the South Lancs. Regiment, has now -been discharged. Baird, 83, Leghstreet, a former brewer at Messrs. Peter Walker's Brewery, has been wounded, -and is now in hospital at Manchester. when I saw it. was out when the paper came, and out when the prize came; but still I thought I had no such luck as winning. I was delighted to know, that I had. won. I have put a brown paper back on the book, and I will take great care of it, and keep it neat.—l remain, your loving' nephew, JOSEPH PERRY." SAVED BY A HYMN. 'Here is an old and striking story from the• " Presbyterian ":—" Many years. ago some Americans in crossing the Attanttia- were singin4 hymns together on a Sunday evening when an exquisitely rich tenor voice joined' in. "Jesus, Lover of My Soul." Another of the singers stopped suddenly to listen, and. when the glorioua voice sang the, verse : All my trust- ow Thee is stayed, All my hetp, from Thee I bring. Cover my defenceless head With the. shadow of Thy wing," the listener said to himself, "By George! It is the very man!" When the service was over ha went up to the man with the glorious tenor voice and asked, "Were you in the Civil War?" "Yes," he answered, "but I fought on the side of the South. I was a Confederate." "Yes, you were a Confederate, and you were posted as a sentinel at such a post on such a night?" "Yes, I was. How do you know?"" Doesn't that hymn you have just sung tell you?" "To be sure," cried the other. "I remember now. I was posted at sentry duty near the edge of a wood on a night that was very cold and dark, and as the enemy were known to be near I felt unnerved, homesick, miserable, and, to tell the whole truth, .afraid. To comfort and encourage myself I sang that verse of .the hymn, All my trust on Thee is stayed,' &c., and felt somehow a strange - peace, come down upon me, which remained with me all that long and lonely night." "Well you might," said the other, "for you owed your life to that verse and your voice. I was a Union soldier, and that night I was in the wood with a party of scouts. I saw you standing on sentry, but could not see your face, and I ordered' my men toi-cover you with their rifles. At this momeAttyOu broke out with that glorious voice and verse, which rang through the still night and seemed to paralyse my men. They looked at me when you came to 'Cover- my defenceless head With the shadow of Thy wing," and I answered the look, All right, boys; lower the rifles,' and we stole back to our lines." THE HAPPIEST BOY. Who is the happiest boy you know? Who has the best time of it? Is it the one who is the very best runner, who has the most marbles, who gets the biggest Score at cricket, who has a good bicycle, or who wears the best clothes? Let us see what this story has to say about it:— Once there was a king who had a little boy whom he loved. He gave him beautiful rooms to live in and pictures and' toys and books. He gave him a pony to ride, a boat to row on a lake, and servants. But for all this the young prince was not happy. He wore a frown wherever he went, and was always wishing for something he did not have. At length one day a magician came to the court. He saw the boy and said to the King, "I can make your son happy. But you must pay me my own price for telling you the secret." "Well," said the King, "what you ask I will So the magician took the boy into a private room. He wrote something with a white sub- stance on a piece of paper. Next he gave the boy a candle and told him to light it and hold it under the paper and then see what he could read. Then he went away and asked no price at all. The boy did as he had been told, and the white letters on the paper turned into a beauti- ful blue. They formed these words : - "DO A KINDNESS TO SOMEONE EVERY DAY." The Prince made -use of the secret and be,ame the happiest boy in the kingdom Willie's Reasoning. Willie: "Papa, if I was twins, would you buy the other boy a banana, too?" Papa: "Certainly, my son." Willie: "Well, papa, you surely ain't going to cheat me Out of another banana just 'cause I'm all in one piece?"

Private Shaw, who is twenty-three years of age joined the Army at the outbreak of war, and has been at the Front twelve months. At the time of enlisting he was employed on the L. and N.-W. His parents have also received the followivg reassuring message from his officer: •`lle is only slightly wounded—in fact, I assure you it is not serious—a piece of shrapnel in the leg. We all envied him as we pictured him having a long rest from the horrors of war and after a little time in hospital seeing old England again. My Only regret is that I have I2st my best gunner. It is so sad to lose one's best man after being out together so long." - Private Wytcherley was one of the first Boy Scouts in Warrington and also a member of the Church Lads' Brigade. He answered his country's call in August, 1914, and, as the letter from jhis officer proves, has "delivered the goods."
